## IDEA Framework Assessment

Using the IDEA Framework (IDEA-Framework-Sept-2023-revised-9-27.pdf) as a reference:

### Paragraph Text — No Red Highlights Applied

No paragraph text was flagged in red. However, the following observations and recommendations are noted:

### Recommended Changes (Non-Text and General)

1. **Diverse Authors, Researchers, and Studies (IDEA 7.4):** Per the IDEA Framework: "Referencing discipline contributors—e.g., researchers, scholars, academics—with backgrounds like those of students both validates and affirms the students as student-scholars and invites them into the academic conversation." The text discusses galaxy formation theory, the black hole-galaxy mass relationship, and large-scale structure formation without naming any researchers. This is an opportunity to mention contributors from diverse backgrounds. For example:
   - The discovery of the black hole-galaxy mass relationship involved contributions from researchers at institutions around the world, including work by Andrea Ghez (University of California, Los Angeles), who shared the 2020 Nobel Prize in Physics for her work on the supermassive black hole at the center of the Milky Way.
   - The Illustris and IllustrisTNG simulations of large-scale structure formation (referenced implicitly in Figure XXX, formerly 28.29) involved researchers from institutions across Europe, North America, and beyond.
   - Per the IDEA Framework: "Include current, more diverse contributors when possible and relevant where historical contributors are not diverse." Consider adding a feature box or sidebar highlighting a researcher from an underrepresented group who has contributed to galaxy formation theory or large-scale structure modeling.

2. **Illustrations and Photos (IDEA 7.1):** All figures in this section are schematic diagrams, telescope images, or computer simulation outputs. There are no images of people. Per the IDEA Framework, consider adding a sidebar or feature box with an image of a researcher from an underrepresented group who has contributed to this field — for example, Andrea Ghez, or a researcher from a non-Western institution working on galaxy formation simulations.

3. **Applications and Examples (IDEA 7.5):** Per the IDEA Framework: "Be mindful when creating exercises that require specific knowledge, context, or frame of reference." Two analogies in the original text were revised (see changes 15 and 16 above):
   - The nightclub analogy was replaced with a concert hall analogy.
   - The Lego analogy was replaced with a building blocks analogy.
   These changes make the text more universally accessible and avoid assumptions about students' familiarity with specific commercial brands or age-restricted cultural contexts.

4. **Incorporating Diverse Perspectives (IDEA Framework, Section 3.0):** The text presents galaxy formation theory primarily through a Western scientific framework. Per the IDEA Framework: "What is claimed as 'history'...is actually a mono-cultural, Western...version of human experience." While the scientific content itself is universal, consider acknowledging that our current understanding of galaxy formation is the result of contributions from researchers across many countries and institutions. For example, the Sloan Digital Sky Survey (discussed in a previous section) involved scientists from institutions in the United States, Japan, Germany, and other countries. The text could briefly note the international and collaborative nature of modern cosmological research.

5. **Appropriate Terminology (IDEA 7.6):** The text uses the phrase "red and dead" to describe elliptical galaxies. This is standard astronomical terminology. However, per the IDEA Framework's guidance on appropriate terminology, consider whether this phrase could carry unintended connotations for some students. It is flagged here as a consideration, not a required change, since it is standard scientific vocabulary.

6. **Knowledge and Representation (IDEA Framework, Section 3.0):** The IDEA Framework notes: "If academic knowledge is almost exclusively provided by authors who share an origin, perspective, or history, we must ask how we can be assured that our knowledge has not been filtered through a specific lens." The content in this section is primarily theoretical and observational cosmology, which is a field where structural barriers have historically limited participation by women, BIPOC researchers, and researchers from non-Western institutions. Per the IDEA Framework's guidance on "emerging inclusivity" as an obtainable goal, even a brief acknowledgment of the international and diverse nature of modern cosmological research would move this section toward greater inclusivity.

## Recommended Section Headers

```
H2: How Galaxies and Large-Scale Structure Form
    [Introductory paragraph: The central question — how did the universe get its current structure?
     The short answer: dark matter + gravity + time]

H3: How Galaxies Form and Grow
    [Top-down vs. bottom-up formation; elliptical and spiral galaxy formation;
     the black hole-galaxy mass relationship; AGN feedback]

H3: Forming Galactic Structures
    [Evidence for early galaxy clusters; dark matter seeds; supercomputer models of
     large-scale structure formation; the cosmic web]

H3: The Story of the Universe
    [Putting it all together: from smooth beginnings to filaments, voids, and clusters;
     bottom-up assembly of galaxies and large-scale structure]
```

## IDEA Framework Assessment

After reviewing the content against the IDEA Framework (IDEA-Framework-Sept-2023-revised-9-27.pdf):

### Paragraph Text — No Red Highlights Applied

No paragraph text was flagged in red. However, the following observations and recommendations are noted based on the IDEA Framework:

### Recommended Changes (Non-Text and General)

1. **Gendered Language (IDEA 7.3 — Gender-Inclusive Language and Use of Pronouns):** The original text contained the sentence: "As many a ship captain in the polar seas found out too late, the part of the iceberg visible above the ocean's surface was not necessarily the only part he needed to pay attention to." This sentence was removed per workflow rule 6 (parenthetical sentence removal). However, it is worth noting that the original contained a gendered pronoun ("he") applied to a generic role. Per IDEA Framework 7.3, generic roles should not default to a single gender. If this analogy is reinstated, it should use gender-neutral language (e.g., "they needed to pay attention to").

2. **Diverse Authors, Researchers, and Studies (IDEA 7.4):** The text mentions Albert Einstein by name in connection with general relativity. Per IDEA Framework 7.4, when individual researchers are named, the text should, where possible, also include contributions from researchers of diverse backgrounds. The history of dark matter research includes important contributions from researchers such as Vera Rubin and Kent Ford (rotation curves), Fritz Zwicky (cluster mass discrepancies), and more recently researchers from institutions across Asia, Latin America, and Africa. Consider adding a brief mention of Vera Rubin's pioneering work on rotation curves, as her contributions are directly relevant to the content of the Dark Matter and Galaxies section and she represents an underrepresented group (women in astrophysics) whose work was historically overlooked. Per the IDEA Framework: "Include current, more diverse contributors when possible and relevant where historical contributors are not diverse."

3. **Illustrations and Photos (IDEA 7.1):** All figures in this section are telescope images, data plots, or composite astronomical images. There are no images of people. Per IDEA Framework 7.1, consider adding a sidebar or feature box that highlights a researcher from an underrepresented group who contributed to dark matter research — for example, Vera Rubin, whose work on galaxy rotation curves provided some of the most compelling evidence for dark matter, or researchers from non-Western institutions currently working on dark matter detection experiments.

4. **Applications and Examples (IDEA Framework):** The analogy used to explain dark matter detection (the ship captain and the iceberg) was removed per workflow rules. The remaining analogies in the text (air particles in a warm vs. cold room for hot/cold dark matter) are accessible and do not assume specific cultural knowledge. Per the IDEA Framework: "Be mindful when creating exercises that require specific knowledge, context, or frame of reference." The air particle analogy is a good example of an inclusive, universally accessible analogy.

5. **Incorporating Diverse Perspectives (IDEA Framework):** The text discusses the history of dark matter discovery primarily through the lens of Western astronomical institutions. Per IDEA Framework section 3.0 and 7.4, consider acknowledging that our current understanding of dark matter is the result of contributions from researchers across many countries and institutions, not solely from Western universities. For example, the Atacama Large Millimeter Array (ALMA) in Chile involves international collaboration, and dark matter research is being conducted at institutions across Asia, Latin America, and beyond.

6. **Appropriate Terminology (IDEA 7.6):** The term "MACHOs" (MAssive Compact Halo Objects) is a standard astronomical acronym. The text also references "WIMPs" (Weakly Interacting Massive Particles) implicitly. These terms are standard in the field and do not carry negative connotations in this context.

## Recommended Section Headers

```
H2: Dark Matter and the Universe
    [Introductory paragraph: Overview of dark matter's importance in galaxy evolution and the universe]

H3: Local Dark Matter
    [Evidence (or lack thereof) for dark matter in the solar system and near the Sun]

H3: Dark Matter and Galaxies
    [Rotation curves; evidence for dark matter halos in spiral galaxies]

H3: Dark Matter in Clusters of Galaxies
    [Galaxy velocities; gravitational lensing; X-ray emission as methods for detecting dark matter in clusters]

H3: Mass-to-Light Ratio
    [Definition and measurements; Table of mass-to-light ratios; dark matter dominance at galaxy scales and above]

H3: Properties of Dark Matter
    [MACHOs; hot vs. cold dark matter; current evidence favoring cold dark matter models]

H3: Dark Energy and the Composition of the Universe
    [Introduction to dark energy; inventory of the universe's contents; open questions in cosmology]
```

## IDEA Framework Assessment

### Paragraph Text Highlighted in Red

- **Margaret Geller and John Huchra paragraph** (Superclusters and Voids section): Highlighted in red. Per IDEA Framework 7.4 (Diverse Authors, Researchers, and Studies), the text names only two researchers without acknowledging the diversity of contributors to large-scale structure research. The IDEA Framework states: "If the contributions are dominated by cis-hetero white men, discuss this with the class and include some of the historical and structural explanations for the lack of diversity" and "Include current, more diverse contributors when possible." Consider supplementing this passage with mention of other contributors to large-scale structure mapping from a wider range of backgrounds and institutions.

### Recommended Changes (Non-Text and General)

1. **Illustrations and Photos (IDEA 7.1):** All figures in this section are telescope images, survey maps, diagrams, and one image of a single male astronomer (Richard Kron, Figure XXX). Per IDEA Framework 7.1, consider adding images that represent a more diverse range of scientists working in this field. The single image of a person in this section shows one individual; consider whether an additional image of a more diverse group of researchers could be included.
2. **Diverse Authors, Researchers, and Studies (IDEA 7.4):** The text names Margaret Geller and John Huchra as pioneers of galaxy mapping. Consider adding a brief mention of other researchers from underrepresented groups who have contributed to large-scale structure surveys, such as contributors to the SDSS from institutions in Asia, Latin America, or other regions. The IDEA Framework specifically notes: "Include examples of and references to historically underrepresented groups, such as Arabic contributors to mathematics and astronomy."
3. **Applications and Examples (IDEA Framework):** The analogy of explorers in "uncharted territory" carries colonial connotations, as many territories described as "uncharted" by Western explorers were in fact well-known to Indigenous peoples. Per IDEA Framework guidance on appropriate terminology, consider revising this analogy to use a more neutral framing, such as scientists mapping an unknown region of the ocean floor or researchers surveying a newly discovered ecosystem.
4. **Appropriate Terminology (IDEA 7.6):** The phrase "uncharted territory" in the Superclusters and Voids section may carry unintended colonial connotations. Consider revising to a more neutral metaphor.
5. **Outdated Content:** The text states that the James Webb Space Telescope "is scheduled to launch" in 2021. The telescope launched on December 25, 2021. This should be updated to reflect the actual launch and subsequent scientific results. This is flagged in orange in the HTML above.
6. **Incorporating Diverse Perspectives (IDEA Framework):** The "Galaxy Zoo" citizen science project is mentioned positively and highlights global participation. This is a strength of the section from an IDEA perspective. Consider expanding this mention to note the global diversity of participants and the accessibility of citizen science as a way to include diverse perspectives in research.

## Recommended Section Headers

```
H2: The Distribution of Galaxies in Space
    [Introductory paragraph: Why galaxy distribution matters for understanding evolution and collisions]

H3: The Cosmological Principle
    [Hubble's survey findings; isotropy and homogeneity; the cosmological principle defined]

H3: The Local Group
    [Our galactic neighborhood; members of the Local Group; recent discoveries of dwarf galaxies]

H3: Neighboring Groups and Clusters
    [Galaxy clusters: poor and rich; the Virgo Cluster; the Coma Cluster; elliptical vs. spiral galaxies in clusters]

H3: Gravitational Lensing
    [How mass bends light; multiple images and arcs; lensing as a tool for probing distant galaxies]

H3: Superclusters and Voids
    [Three-dimensional mapping; filaments and superclusters; voids; large-scale structure of the universe]

H3: The Sloan Digital Sky Survey
    [Technology and methods of the SDSS; results and data scale; citizen science and Galaxy Zoo]
```

## IDEA Framework Assessment

After reviewing the content against the IDEA Framework (IDEA-Framework-Sept-2023-revised-9-27.pdf):

**No paragraph text was flagged in red.** The content is primarily scientific and technical. However, the following observations are noted:

### Recommended Changes (Non-Text and General)

1. **Illustrations and Photos (IDEA 7.1):** All figures in this section are telescope images of galaxies, simulations, or composite data visualizations. There are no images of people. Per the IDEA Framework, consider adding a sidebar, feature box, or supplementary image that highlights a researcher from an underrepresented group who has contributed to the study of galaxy collisions, mergers, or AGN — for example, a researcher from a non-Western institution or a woman in astrophysics.
2. **Diverse Authors, Researchers, and Studies (IDEA 7.4):** The figure credits mention researchers by name (e.g., P. Jonsson, G. Novak, T. J. Cox, Pieter van Dokkum, Marijn Franx). All credited researchers appear to be from Western institutions. Consider adding a feature box or note that highlights contributions from researchers at institutions in other parts of the world, or from underrepresented groups, who have advanced our understanding of galaxy evolution and mergers.
3. **Incorporating Diverse Perspectives (IDEA Framework):** The text uses the analogy of "two equal companies that join forces" (from the business world) to explain galaxy mergers. This is a culturally specific analogy rooted in Western corporate culture. Consider offering an alternative or additional analogy that is more globally accessible.
4. **Appropriate Terminology (IDEA 7.6):** The term "galactic cannibalism" is a colorful astronomical term that uses the metaphor of consumption. While it is standard terminology in the field, consider adding a brief note acknowledging that it is a metaphor, particularly given the historical and colonial connotations of the word "cannibalism." The IDEA Framework recommends identifying terminology that may carry unintended negative connotations and providing context where appropriate.
5. **Applications and Examples (IDEA Framework):** The grapefruit/San Francisco analogy for star separation is effective and accessible. Consider whether additional analogies from non-U.S. geographic contexts could be offered as alternatives to make the content more globally inclusive.

# Figure 16.5.3 — Expanding Raisin Bread

## 1. Accessible Description

```html
<h2>Figure 16.5.3 Expanding Raisin Bread</h2>
<p>A diagram of two loaves of raisin bread, one unrisen and one expanded, showing raisins moving farther apart.</p>
<h3>Unrisen Loaf (30 cm)</h3>
<ul>
    <li>A smaller loaf of bread, 30 cm wide, with several raisins scattered through its right half.</li>
    <li>A reference raisin near the upper left of the raisin-filled region has arrows pointing to four other raisins, each labeled with its distance from the reference.</li>
    <li>Clockwise from upper left, the distances are 3 cm, 16 cm, 20 cm, and 7 cm.</li>
</ul>
<h3>Risen Loaf (60 cm)</h3>
<ul>
    <li>A larger loaf of bread, 60 cm wide, twice the width of the unrisen loaf, with the same raisins now spread farther apart.</li>
    <li>The same reference raisin has arrows to the same four raisins, with the distances increased proportionally.</li>
    <li>Clockwise from upper left, the distances are now 6 cm, 32 cm, 40 cm, and 14 cm.</li>
</ul>
<h3>Key Takeaway</h3>
<ul>
    <li>Every distance from the reference raisin has doubled as the loaf doubled in width, so raisins that started farther away end up moving away faster than raisins that started close by.</li>
</ul>
```

## 2. New Figure Caption Text

As the loaf rises uniformly, every raisin's distance from a reference raisin doubles, showing why more distant raisins, like more distant galaxies, appear to recede faster during uniform expansion.

## 3. New Alt-Text

Diagram of expanding raisin bread showing raisin distances doubling as the loaf rises. Details in caption.

## 4. Original Alt-Text & Caption (preserved)

- **Original alt-text:** Expanding Raisin Bread. In this illustration, the loaf of raisin bread at left is 30 cm wide. Arrows are drawn from a reference raisin located to the left of center to four other raisins within the loaf, with distances indicated. Clockwise from upper left: 3 cm, 16 cm, 25 cm and 7 cm from the reference. At right, the loaf has expanded to 60 cm wide. The distances from the reference raisin have increased accordingly. Clockwise from upper left: 6 cm, 32 cm, 50 cm and 14 cm.
- **Original caption text:** Expanding Raisin Bread. As the raisin bread rises, the raisins "see" other raisins moving away. More distant raisins move away faster in a uniformly expanding bread.

# Figure 16.5.2 — Stretching a Ruler

## 1. Accessible Description

```html
<h2>Figure 16.5.2 Stretching a Ruler</h2>
<p>A diagram of two rulers, one normal and one stretched, illustrating how ants on the stretched ruler move apart.</p>
<h3>Top Ruler (Normal Length)</h3>
<ul>
    <li>A short wooden ruler marked with tick marks labeled 0 through 12 in increments of 2.</li>
    <li>Four ants sit on the ruler, positioned above the marks at 2, 4, 7, and 12.</li>
</ul>
<h3>Bottom Ruler (Stretched)</h3>
<ul>
    <li>A longer wooden ruler, stretched to about twice the length of the top ruler, with arrows at both ends indicating the stretching.</li>
    <li>The same tick marks, labeled 0 through 12, are now spaced twice as far apart as on the top ruler.</li>
    <li>The four ants remain positioned above the same numbers, 2, 4, 7, and 12, but are now spread farther apart in physical space because the ruler has stretched.</li>
    <li>Three labeled arrows beneath the ruler show the speed at which each pair of neighboring ants moves apart, as seen by the ant at position 2:
    <ul>
        <li>Between 2 and 4: 2 cm/min.</li>
        <li>Between 4 and 7: 5 cm/min.</li>
        <li>Between 7 and 12: 10 cm/min.</li>
    </ul>
    </li>
</ul>
<h3>Key Takeaway</h3>
<ul>
    <li>The farther apart two ants are on the ruler, the faster they appear to move away from each other as the ruler stretches, illustrating that recession speed is proportional to distance.</li>
</ul>
```

## 2. New Figure Caption Text

As the ruler stretches uniformly, ants farther apart move away from each other faster than ants close together, an analogy for how the expansion of space causes more distant galaxies to recede at greater speeds.

## 3. New Alt-Text

Diagram of ants on a stretching ruler, showing recession speed increasing with distance. Details in caption.

## 4. Original Alt-Text & Caption (preserved)

- **Original alt-text:** Stretching a Ruler. In this illustration, the ruler at top is normal sized, and has ants drawn at 2, 4, 7 and 12 cm. The same ruler is drawn below, but stretched to twice its length. The ants are still at their positions as above, but the ant at 2 cm sees the ant at 4 cm move away at 2 cm/min, the ant at 7 cm move away at 5 cm/min and the ant at 12 cm move away at 10 cm/min.
- **Original caption text:** Stretching a Ruler. Ants on a stretching ruler see other ants move away from them. The speed with which another ant moves away is proportional to its distance.

# Figure 16.5.1 — Hubble's Law

## 1. Accessible Description

```html
<h2>Figure 16.5.1 Hubble's Law</h2>
<p>Two charts comparing Hubble's early velocity-distance data to a later, more extensive dataset.</p>
<h3>Chart (a): Hubble's Data (1929)</h3>
<ul>
    <li>The vertical axis is labeled Velocity (km/s) and goes from 0 at the bottom to 1500 at the top, in increments of 500 km/s.</li>
    <li>The horizontal axis is labeled Distance (millions of LY) and goes from 0 on the left to 6 on the right, in increments of 3 million light-years.</li>
    <li>Red dots show individual galaxy measurements, scattered broadly around a general upward trend, with substantial spread at any given distance.</li>
    <li>A red line representing the average trend runs from about 0 million LY, 0 km/s at the lower left to about 6 million LY, 1000 km/s at the upper right.</li>
</ul>
<h3>Chart (b): Hubble and Humason (1931)</h3>
<ul>
    <li>The vertical axis is labeled Velocity (km/s) and goes from 0 at the bottom to 20,000 at the top, in increments of 5,000 km/s.</li>
    <li>The horizontal axis is labeled Distance (millions of LY) and goes from 0 on the left to 120 on the right, in increments of 30 million light-years.</li>
    <li>Black dots show individual galaxy measurements, following a tight, nearly straight upward trend extending out to about 100 million light-years and 19,000 km/s.</li>
    <li>A black line representing the average trend runs from about 0 million LY, 0 km/s at the lower left to about 100 million LY, 19,000 km/s at the upper right.</li>
    <li>The red data points from Chart (a) are also plotted here, clustered near the lower left corner, showing how limited the 1929 dataset was compared to the 1931 dataset.</li>
</ul>
<h3>Key Trend</h3>
<ul>
    <li>Both charts show that galaxy velocity increases with distance, but the 1931 dataset extends to distances and velocities roughly 20 times greater than the 1929 dataset, with a much tighter, more clearly linear relationship.</li>
</ul>
```

## 2. New Figure Caption Text

Comparing Hubble's 1929 data to Hubble and Humason's 1931 data shows how quickly astronomers extended the known range of galaxy distances and velocities, strengthening the case for a linear velocity-distance relation.

## 3. New Alt-Text

Two charts of galaxy velocity vs. distance: Hubble's 1929 data and the more extensive 1931 dataset. Details in caption.

## 4. Original Alt-Text & Caption (preserved)

- **Original alt-text:** Hubble's Law. The plot in panel (a), at left, is labeled Hubble's Data (1929) and has the vertical axis labeled Velocity (km/s), running from -500 at bottom to 1500 at top in increments of 500 km/s. The horizontal axis is labeled Distance (millions of LY), and runs from zero at left to 6 at right in increments of 3 million light years. The data is plotted as red dots, with the mean drawn as a red line beginning at 0 LY, 0 km/s at lower left to 6 MLY, 1000 km/s at upper right. The plot in panel (b), at right, is labeled Hubble and Humason (1931) and has the vertical axis labeled Velocity (km/s), running from zero at bottom to 20,000 at top in increments of 5,000 km/s. The horizontal axis is labeled Distance (millions of LY), and runs from zero at left to 120 at right in increments of 30 million LY. The data is plotted as black dots, with the mean drawn as a black line beginning at 0 LY, 0 km/s at lower left to 100 MLY, 19,000 km/s at upper right. The data from panel (a) are plotted in red at lower left.
- **Original caption text:** Hubble's Law. (a) These data show Hubble's original velocity-distance relation, adapted from his 1929 paper in the Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences. (b) These data show Hubble and Humason's velocity-distance relation, adapted from their 1931 paper in The Astrophysical Journal. The red dots at the lower left are the points in the diagram in the 1929 paper. Comparison of the two graphs shows how rapidly the determination of galactic distances and redshifts progressed in the 2 years between these publications.

# Figure 16.4.2 — Type Ia Supernova

## 1. Accessible Description

```html
<h2>Figure 16.4.2 Type Ia Supernova</h2>
<p>An image of a type Ia supernova outshining its host spiral galaxy.</p>
<h3>Image Elements</h3>
<ul>
    <li>A very bright, white star-like point of light with prominent diffraction spikes sits left of center, lower than the galaxy.</li>
    <li>This bright point is the type Ia supernova, appearing far brighter than any individual star in the surrounding field.</li>
    <li>A spiral galaxy is in the upper right of the image, oriented at an angle, with a bright central bulge and dusty, reddish-brown spiral arms winding outward.</li>
    <li>A second, fainter bright star with diffraction spikes appears near the top of the galaxy, above and to the left of its center.</li>
    <li>The supernova lies at the outer edge of the galaxy's spiral arms, appearing much brighter than the galaxy's own bright core despite being part of the same system.</li>
    <li>The background is filled with numerous faint, distant galaxies of varying shapes, along with scattered foreground stars.</li>
</ul>
<p>Credit: NASA, ESA, A. Riess (STScI)</p>
```

## 2. New Figure Caption Text

Near its peak brightness, this type Ia supernova outshines its entire host galaxy, a predictable peak luminosity that lets astronomers use these events as standard candles to measure cosmic distances.

## 3. New Alt-Text

Type Ia supernova outshining its host spiral galaxy from the galaxy's outskirts. Details in caption.

## 4. Original Alt-Text & Caption (preserved)

- **Original alt-text:** Type Ia Supernova. The very bright star to the left of center is a type Ia supernova at the outskirts of the spiral galaxy seen at upper right.
- **Original caption text:** Type Ia Supernova. The bright object at the bottom left of center is a type Ia supernova near its peak intensity. The supernova easily outshines its host galaxy. This extreme increase and luminosity help astronomers use Ia supernova as standard bulbs.

# Figure 16.4.1 — Cepheid Variable Star in M100

## 1. Accessible Description

```html
<h2>Figure 16.4.1 Cepheid Variable Star</h2>
<p>A Hubble Space Telescope image of the galaxy M100 with three close-up insets tracking a variable star's brightness over time.</p>
<h3>Background Image</h3>
<ul>
    <li>A grayscale image shows part of the spiral galaxy M100, with bright, dense structure toward the lower left and fainter, thinner spiral arms extending toward the upper right.</li>
    <li>A small white square near the center right of the image marks the location of the Cepheid variable star.</li>
    <li>Two thin lines extend upward from the square, connecting it to the two closest inset panels above.</li>
</ul>
<h3>May 4</h3>
<ul>
    <li>Top left inset panel.</li>
    <li>A close-up, grainy, black-and-white view of the marked region.</li>
    <li>The target star is faint and difficult to distinguish from the surrounding scattered points of light.</li>
</ul>
<h3>May 9</h3>
<ul>
    <li>Top center inset panel.</li>
    <li>The same region as the May 4 panel, showing the target star slightly brighter and easier to distinguish near the center of the panel.</li>
</ul>
<h3>May 31</h3>
<ul>
    <li>Top right inset panel.</li>
    <li>The same region again, with the target star now clearly the brightest point in the panel, standing out against the surrounding fainter stars.</li>
</ul>
<p>Credit: modification of work by Wendy L. Freedman, Observatories of the Carnegie Institution of Washington, and NASA/ESA</p>
```

## 2. New Figure Caption Text

Hubble Space Telescope images taken on three separate nights track the changing brightness of a single Cepheid variable star in M100, a method astronomers used to measure the galaxy's distance at 56 million light-years.

## 3. New Alt-Text

Cepheid variable star in galaxy M100, shown brightening across insets from May 4 to May 31. Details in caption.

## 4. Original Alt-Text & Caption (preserved)

- **Original alt-text:** Cepheid Variable Star in M100. In the background of this image is a portion of the galaxy M100. At center right is a small white box indicating the area that contains the variable star observed using the Hubble Space Telescope. Along the top of the image are three insets showing the star at three different times. From left: May 4, May 9 and May 31. The star is significantly brighter in the May 31 image.
- **Original caption text:** Cepheid Variable Star. In 1994, using the Hubble Space Telescope, astronomers were able to make out an individual cepheid variable star in the galaxy M100 and measure its distance to be 56 million light-years. The insets show the star on three different nights; you can see that its brightness is indeed variable.

# Figure 16.3.1 — The Pinwheel Galaxy (M101)

## 1. Accessible Description

```html
<h2>Figure 16.3.1 The Pinwheel Galaxy</h2>
<p>A face-on image of a spiral galaxy taken by the Hubble Space Telescope.</p>
<h3>Image Elements</h3>
<ul>
    <li>The galaxy fills most of the frame, oriented face-on so its full spiral structure is visible.</li>
    <li>A bright, compact, yellowish-white core sits near the center of the galaxy.</li>
    <li>Multiple spiral arms wind outward from the core in a pinwheel pattern, tightly wound near the center and loosening toward the outer edge.</li>
    <li>The spiral arms are traced by reddish-brown dust lanes and clusters of bright blue points marking regions of active star formation.</li>
    <li>The outer regions of the galaxy fade into faint, diffuse blue light with wispy, irregular edges rather than a sharp boundary.</li>
    <li>A patch of clumpy blue material extends from the upper left edge of the galaxy, appearing separate from the main spiral structure.</li>
    <li>Numerous foreground stars are scattered across the black background, appearing as small white, blue, and orange points of light.</li>
    <li>Several faint, fuzzy smudges of light in the background are distant galaxies.</li>
</ul>
<p>Credit: NASA, ESA, K. Kuntz (Johns Hopkins University), F. Bresolin (University of Hawaii), J. Trauger (Jet Propulsion Lab), J. Mould (NOAO), Y.-H. Chu (University of Illinois, Urbana), and STScI</p>
```

## 2. New Figure Caption Text

This visible-light image from the Hubble Space Telescope shows the well-ordered spiral arms of a face-on galaxy, the kind of structure astronomers study to measure rotation curves and detect the presence of dark matter.

## 3. New Alt-Text

Face-on spiral galaxy M101, the Pinwheel Galaxy, with bright core and blue star-forming arms. Details in caption.

## 4. Original Alt-Text & Caption (preserved)

- **Original alt-text:** The Pinwheel Galaxy M101. A prime example of a large, face-on spiral galaxy.
- **Original caption text:** The Pinwheel Galaxy. This galaxy is a face-on spiral at a distance of 21 million light-years. M101 is almost twice the diameter of the Milky Way, and it contains at least 1 trillion stars.

<h2>Figure 16.2.6 Large Magellanic Cloud</h2>
<p>A wide-field image of the irregular dwarf galaxy the Large Magellanic Cloud.</p>
<h3>Image Elements</h3>
<ul>
    <li>A large, elongated, hazy cloud of stars and gas stretches diagonally across the center of the image, brighter and denser toward the middle.</li>
    <li>The central region glows with a soft pink and white haze, dotted with many individual stars.</li>
    <li>A bright, colorful clustering of stars and glowing gas, the Tarantula Nebula, appears as a distinct bright patch to the upper left of the galaxy's center.</li>
    <li>Numerous smaller clusters of blue and red stars are scattered throughout the galaxy, marking regions of active star formation.</li>
    <li>The galaxy has no organized spiral structure, giving it an irregular, patchy shape.</li>
    <li>A dense field of foreground stars in various colors, blue, white, orange, and red, fills the surrounding dark background.</li>
</ul>
```

The Large Magellanic Cloud, one of the Milky Way's nearest galactic neighbors, is an irregular dwarf galaxy whose bright star-forming regions, including the Tarantula Nebula, make it a valuable target for studying stellar evolution and galactic dynamics.

    <li>This structure suggests the galaxy formed relatively recently from the collision of two spiral galaxies.</li>

</ul>

<h3>Image Elements</h3>
<ul>
    <li>A large, bright spiral galaxy seen nearly face-on, filling most of the frame against a dark background of scattered stars.</li>
    <li>A straight, elongated bar of stars and dust extends through the center of the galaxy, rather than a rounded central bulge.</li>
    <li>Two spiral arms begin at the ends of the bar, curving outward and wrapping around the galaxy.</li>
    <li>The arms are lined with reddish dust lanes near the bar and lighter blue clusters of young stars farther out.</li>
    <li>A small, bright yellow-white nucleus sits at the center of the bar.</li>
    <li>Credit: NASA, ESA, and the Hubble Heritage Team (STScI/AURA)</li>
</ul>
```

## 1. Accessible Description

```html
<h2>Figure 16.2.1 Classification of Galaxies</h2>
<p>Diagram illustrating Hubble's tuning fork classification of galaxies.</p>
<h3>Ellipticals</h3>
<ul>
    <li>A row of five shapes runs along the left side of the diagram, connected by a blue line, labeled Ellipticals.</li>
    <li>From left to right, the shapes progress from round to increasingly flattened ovals, labeled E0, E3, E5, E7, and S0.</li>
    <li>E0 is a circular shape.</li>
    <li>E3 and E5 are progressively flatter ovals.</li>
    <li>E7 is a very elongated, thin oval.</li>
    <li>S0 is a more circular shape with a bright core, marking the transition point where the line splits into two branches.</li>
    <li>Below each labeled shape is an image of a real elliptical galaxy of that type, shown as a fuzzy, glowing patch of light without spiral structure.</li>
</ul>
<h3>Spirals</h3>
<ul>
    <li>From the S0 transition point, the upper branch of the line curves to the right and is labeled Spirals.</li>
    <li>Three illustrated spiral shapes are spaced along this branch, labeled Sa, Sb, and Sc from left to right.</li>
    <li>Sa has short, tightly wound spiral arms around a large central bulge.</li>
    <li>Sb has longer arms and a smaller central bulge.</li>
    <li>Sc has long, open, multiple spiral arms and a small central bulge.</li>
    <li>Above each illustrated type is an image of a real spiral galaxy of that type.</li>
</ul>
<h3>Barred Spirals</h3>
<ul>
    <li>From the S0 transition point, the lower branch of the line curves downward and to the right, forming the second prong of the tuning fork.</li>
    <li>Three illustrated barred spiral shapes are spaced along this branch, labeled SBa, SBb, and SBc from left to right.</li>
    <li>SBa has a short central bar with tightly wound arms extending from its ends.</li>
    <li>SBb has a longer bar with more open arms.</li>
    <li>SBc has a long, prominent bar with long, open arms extending from its ends.</li>
    <li>Below each illustrated type is an image of a real barred spiral galaxy of that type.</li>
</ul>
<p>Credit: modification of work by NASA, ESA</p>
```

## 2. New Figure Caption Text
Edwin Hubble's tuning fork diagram organizes galaxies by shape, branching from elliptical galaxies into two sequences of spiral and barred spiral galaxies with increasingly open arms.

## 3. New Alt-Text
```
Hubble tuning fork diagram classifying elliptical, spiral, and barred spiral galaxies. Details in caption.
```

## 4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
- **Original alt-text:** Hubble Classification of Galaxies. Sometimes referred to as the tuning fork diagram, this figure illustrates Hubble's classification of galaxies. From left to right are the Ellipticals, beginning with the spherical E0, slightly oval E3, flatter oval E5 to the very elongated oval E7. Below each illustrated type is an image of a galaxy of that type. At center is the more circular S0, from which the line splits into two parts (hence the tuning fork reference) and labeled Spirals. Above are the regular spirals. From left to right beginning with the short armed Sa, longer armed Sb and the open, multiple armed Sc. Above each illustrated type is an image of a galaxy of that type. Below are the barred spirals. From left to right beginning with the short bar SBa, longer bar SBb and the open, long bar SBc. Below each illustrated type is an image of a galaxy of that type.
- **Original caption text:** This figure shows Edwin Hubble's original classification of galaxies. Elliptical galaxies are on the left. On the right, you can see the basic spiral shapes illustrated, alongside images of actual barred and unbarred spirals. (credit: modification of work by NASA, ESA)

## 1. Accessible Description

```html
<h2>Figure 16.1.3 Andromeda Galaxy</h2>
<p>Visible light image of the Andromeda Galaxy and two satellite galaxies.</p>
<h3>Image Elements</h3>
<ul>
    <li>The Andromeda Galaxy, also known as M31, is a large spiral galaxy that fills most of the frame.</li>
    <li>The galaxy's disk is tilted and appears elongated, stretching from the upper left to the lower right of the image.</li>
    <li>A bright, glowing central bulge, or nucleus, marks the middle of the galaxy.</li>
    <li>Reddish-brown dust lanes form a mottled, ring-like pattern around the central bulge.</li>
    <li>Faint blue spiral arms extend outward from the center, dotted with clusters of stars.</li>
    <li>Thousands of foreground stars from our own galaxy are scattered across the black background, appearing as small points of white, blue, and orange light.</li>
    <li>A smaller, round satellite galaxy, M32, appears as a compact glowing patch above and to the right of the galaxy's center.</li>
    <li>A second satellite galaxy, M110, appears as an elongated glowing patch below the main galaxy.</li>
</ul>
<p>Credit: Adam Evans</p>
```

## 2. New Figure Caption Text
The disk, spiral arms, and dust lanes of the Andromeda Galaxy are shown alongside two of its satellite galaxies, illustrating the structure typical of large spiral galaxies like our own.

## 3. New Alt-Text
```
Andromeda Galaxy with satellite galaxies M32 and M110. Details in caption.
```

## 4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
- **Original alt-text:** The Andromeda Galaxy. The compact nucleus of our nearest large galactic neighbor is seen at the center of this visible light image, with the blue spiral arms and thick dust lanes circling around the outer parts of the galaxy.
- **Original caption text:** Also known by its catalog number M31, the Andromeda galaxy is a large spiral galaxy very similar in appearance to, and slightly larger than, our own Galaxy. At a distance of about 2.5 million light-years, Andromeda is the spiral galaxy that is nearest to our own in space. Here, it is seen with two of its satellite galaxies, M32 (top) and M110 (bottom). (credit: Adam Evans)

I looked at the drawing — it's Lord Rosse's famous 1845 sketch of M51, made with the Leviathan of Parsonstown telescope at Birr Castle, and it was the first drawing to reveal a spiral nebula's spiral structure. That companion patch near the upper right corresponds to the companion galaxy now known as NGC 5195.

## IDEA Framework Recommendations

Based on the IDEA Framework (IDEA-Framework-Sept-2023-revised-9-27.pdf):

1. **Diverse Authors, Researchers, and Studies (7.4) — Named Scientists:** The text names only male scientists — Percival Lowell, Vesto Slipher, Georges Lemaître, Alexander Friedmann, Edwin Hubble, Milton Humason, and Harlow Shapley. Per the IDEA Framework, "referencing discipline contributors with backgrounds like those of students both validates and affirms the students as student-scholars and invites them into the academic conversation" (IDEA Framework, 7.4). The IDEA Framework also notes that "if the contributions are dominated by cis-hetero white men, discuss this with the class and include some the historical and structural explanations for the lack of diversity" (IDEA Framework, 7.4). Consider adding a sidebar or note acknowledging that the early 20th-century astronomical community was largely exclusionary, and highlighting contributions from astronomers of diverse backgrounds to the study of galaxy velocities and the expanding universe.

2. **Diverse Authors, Researchers, and Studies (7.4) — Henrietta Swan Leavitt:** The text relies heavily on Cepheid variable measurements without acknowledging Henrietta Swan Leavitt, whose discovery of the period-luminosity relationship for Cepheid variables made Hubble's distance measurements possible. Leavitt was a woman working as a "computer" at the Harvard Observatory, a role that was largely invisible in the scientific record. The IDEA Framework calls for including "examples of and references to historically underrepresented groups" (IDEA Framework, 7.4). Consider adding a sentence acknowledging Leavitt's foundational contribution.

3. **Incorporating Diverse Perspectives (7.8) — Lemaître's Recognition:** The text does note that the International Astronomical Union suggested renaming Hubble's law the Hubble-Lemaître law, which is a positive step toward recognitive justice (IDEA Framework, 3.0). However, the text still refers to the relationship as "Hubble's law" throughout. Consider consistently using "Hubble-Lemaître law" or adding a note at first use explaining the naming history more fully.

4. **Appropriate Terminology (7.6) — STEM Guidance:** The IDEA Framework notes that "referencing theory and concepts by the scientist's or theorist's name elevates the person over the concept" (IDEA Framework, 7.4). Consider whether "Hubble's law" could be supplemented with a descriptive name such as the "Velocity-Distance Law" or "Recession Law" to make the concept more accessible and less dependent on name recognition, particularly given the naming dispute around Lemaître's contributions.

5. **Prior Knowledge Assumptions (7.5):** The raisin bread analogy assumes familiarity with bread-making and yeast. The IDEA Framework cautions against assuming students have cooked when discussing heating or cooling (IDEA Framework, 7.5). Consider briefly explaining what yeast does in bread-making, or using a more universally accessible analogy. Similarly, the reference to "the fourth of July" in the previous section assumes familiarity with U.S. cultural traditions.

6. **Figures — Diverse Representation:** The two analogy figures (stretching ruler with ants, expanding raisin bread) are diagrams with no human subjects. No diversity concerns apply to the images themselves. However, the use of a gendered pronoun ("He measures how fast other ants...") in the ruler analogy paragraph assigns a male gender to the "intelligent ant." Consider using a gender-neutral pronoun such as "they" to model inclusive language.

## IDEA Framework Recommendations

Based on the IDEA Framework (IDEA-Framework-Sept-2023-revised-9-27.pdf):

1. **Diverse Authors, Researchers, and Studies (7.4):** The text names only male scientists — Hubble, Baade, Tully, and Fisher — as contributors to the methods of measuring galactic distances. Per the IDEA Framework, referencing discipline contributors with diverse backgrounds "validates and affirms students as student-scholars and invites them into the academic conversation" (IDEA Framework, 7.4). Consider adding a sentence or sidebar acknowledging the contributions of Wendy L. Freedman (already credited in the figure caption), who led the Hubble Space Telescope Key Project on the extragalactic distance scale, and Henrietta Swan Leavitt, whose discovery of the period-luminosity relationship for Cepheid variables made all of these distance measurements possible.

2. **Incorporating Diverse Perspectives (7.8):** The text centers the history of distance measurement entirely within a Western scientific tradition. Consider adding a brief note acknowledging that Indigenous and non-Western astronomical traditions have long observed and tracked celestial objects, and that modern astronomy builds on a global history of sky observation.

3. **Appropriate Terminology (7.6) — STEM Guidance:** The IDEA Framework notes that for STEM courses, "referencing theory and concepts by the scientist's or theorist's name elevates the person over the concept. When appropriate, shift attention to the concept by using descriptions that signify the reason the concept is important" (IDEA Framework, 7.4). Consider whether "Tully-Fisher relation" could be supplemented with a descriptive name such as the "Rotation-Luminosity Relation" to make the concept more accessible and less dependent on name recognition.

4. **Applications and Problem Scenarios (IDEA Framework):** The analogy about getting to class on time (removed per workflow) assumed a specific student experience. The IDEA Framework cautions against assumptions about prior knowledge or frame of reference. The removal of this analogy aligns with IDEA best practices.

5. **Figures — Diverse Representation:** The figure caption for the Cepheid Variable Star credits Wendy L. Freedman, a prominent woman astronomer. This is a positive example of diverse attribution. Consider adding a brief note in the text about Freedman's role in the HST Key Project to make this contribution more visible to students.

6. **Table XXX (Distance Methods):** The table is clear and well-structured. No IDEA concerns apply to the table content itself.

## IDEA Framework Recommendations

Based on the IDEA Framework (IDEA-Framework-Sept-2023-revised-9-27.pdf), the following recommendations are made:

1. **Diverse Authors, Researchers, and Studies (7.4):** The text references only Edwin Hubble and Kepler by name. The IDEA Framework notes that OER texts still skew heavily toward white male scientists (Thiede, 2021). Consider adding a sentence or sidebar that acknowledges contributions from diverse astronomers to the study of galaxy masses, dark matter, and rotation curves — for example, Vera Rubin and Kent Ford, whose work on galaxy rotation curves was foundational to our understanding of dark matter. This is also an opportunity to highlight the work of researchers from underrepresented groups.

2. **Incorporating Diverse Perspectives (7.8) — Highlighted in Red:** The final paragraph centers Edwin Hubble as the reference point for a historical parallel about scientific breakthroughs. While historically accurate, the text does not acknowledge that the scientific community of Hubble's era was largely exclusionary. The IDEA Framework recommends inserting context or attribution for historical references as needed. Consider adding a brief note acknowledging that the scientific community of today is more diverse than in Hubble's time, and that this diversity strengthens the field.

3. **Illustrations and Photos (7.1):** The single figure in this section (M101, the Pinwheel Galaxy) is an astronomical image with no human subjects. No diversity concerns apply to the image itself. However, the credited researchers in the caption (Kuntz, Bresolin, Trauger, Mould, Chu) represent some diversity. Consider adding a brief note about any of these researchers' backgrounds to model diverse scientific contributions.

4. **Applications and Problem Scenarios (IDEA Framework):** The text uses the Sun as the reference unit for mass and luminosity throughout. This is standard scientific practice and is inclusive. No changes needed here.

5. **Keyword and Glossary Representation (7.7):** The term "dark matter" is used extensively but not linked to a glossary or key terms page. Consider adding a glossary link for this term, as it is a high-priority concept for students and represents an area of active research with contributions from diverse scientists worldwide.

## IDEA Framework Recommendations (Non-Text Material)

1. **Figures — Diverse Representation:** The figures in this section (NGC 2787, M100, NGC 4565, NGC 1300, ESO 325-G004, M32, Large Magellanic Cloud) are all astronomical images with no human subjects. No diversity concerns apply to the images themselves. However, all credited scientists and researchers named in captions (Judy Schmidt, "Jschulman555") appear to represent a limited demographic. Consider adding captions or supplementary text that highlights contributions from a more diverse range of astronomers.
2. **Figures — Caption Attribution:** The Hubble Classification figure credits only NASA and ESA. Consider adding context about Edwin Hubble's background and noting that galaxy classification has been refined by many astronomers from diverse backgrounds since Hubble's time.
3. **Hubble's Legacy:** The text centers Edwin Hubble as the primary figure in galaxy classification without acknowledging other contributors. Consider adding a sentence or note about other astronomers, including women such as Henrietta Swan Leavitt or Cecilia Payne-Gaposchkin, whose work was foundational to understanding galaxies.
4. **Magellanic Clouds — Indigenous Knowledge:** The text notes that Ferdinand Magellan and his crew "were the first European travelers to notice" the Magellanic Clouds. This framing erases the long-standing knowledge of these objects by Indigenous peoples of the Southern Hemisphere, including Aboriginal Australians and peoples of the Pacific Islands and South America, who had names and cultural knowledge of these objects long before European contact. Consider revising this sentence to acknowledge Indigenous astronomical knowledge.

Chapter 15 Figure Pipeline: Collision with Andromeda
1. Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 15.6.5 Collision with Andromeda</h2>

<p>A six-panel artist's illustration sequence showing the future collision between the Milky Way and Andromeda galaxies, as seen from a point 25,000 light-years from the Milky Way's center.</p>

<h3>Panel 1</h3>

<ul>
    <li>3.75 billion years from now.</li>
    <li>The Andromeda galaxy looms large in the night sky, its spiral disk and bright core clearly visible above a dark, rocky horizon.</li>
    <li>The Milky Way's own hazy band of stars stretches across the rest of the sky.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Panel 2</h3>

<ul>
    <li>3.85 billion years from now.</li>
    <li>The interaction has begun. Both galaxies appear visibly distorted, with orange dust lanes and reddish-pink patches of new star formation spreading across the sky as Andromeda draws closer.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Panel 3</h3>

<ul>
    <li>3.9 billion years from now.</li>
    <li>Star formation continues intensifying. The sky is filled with bright clusters, dust clouds, and reddish-pink star-forming regions covering nearly the entire view.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Panel 4</h3>

<ul>
    <li>About 4 billion years from now.</li>
    <li>The galaxy shapes continue to change as they interact. Andromeda appears stretched, and the Milky Way's own structure becomes visibly warped, with sweeping arcs of stars and dust.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Panel 5</h3>

<ul>
    <li>5.1 billion years from now.</li>
    <li>The cores of the two galaxies have become two large, bright, glowing lobes that fill much of the sky, with fainter surrounding structure and scattered star clusters.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Panel 6</h3>

<ul>
    <li>7 billion years from now.</li>
    <li>The merged galaxies now form a single, huge, glowing elliptical mass of stars whose brightness dominates the entire night sky.</li>
</ul>
2. New Figure Caption Text
Over roughly 3 to 4 billion years, the Milky Way and Andromeda will collide, separate, and merge again, eventually forming a single elliptical galaxy, though the collision will also reshuffle the orbits of many stars within each galaxy.

3. New Alt-Text
Six-panel sequence of the Milky Way–Andromeda collision. (+ "Details in caption.")

4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
Original alt-text: Collision of the Milky Way with Andromeda. In panel 1, at upper left, the Andromeda galaxy looms large in the night sky. In panel 2, at top center, the interaction has begun with the Milky Way and Andromeda becoming visibly distorted as Andromeda gets closer to us. In panel 3, at upper right, the sky is ablaze with star forming regions and a riot of dust clouds and star clusters. In panel 4, at lower left, the galaxies further lose their spiral shapes, but dust lanes and star formation persists. By panel 5, at lower center, the two galactic nuclei fill the sky. Finally, in panel 6 at lower right, the nuclei have merged into a huge elliptical mass of stars.

Original caption text: Collision with Andromeda. In about 3 billion years, the Milky Way Galaxy and Andromeda Galaxy will begin a long process of colliding, separating, and then coming back together to form an elliptical galaxy. The whole interaction will take 3 to 4 billion years. These computer-simulated images show the following sequence: (1) In 3.75 billion years, Andromeda has approached the Milky Way. (2) New star formation fills the sky 3.85 billion years from now. (3) Star formation continues at 3.9 billion years. (4) The galaxy shapes change as they interact, with Andromeda being stretched and our Galaxy becoming warped, about 4 billion years from now. (5) In 5.1 billion years, the cores of the two galaxies are bright lobes. (6) In 7 billion years, the merged galaxies form a huge elliptical galaxy whose brightness fills the night sky. This artist's illustrations show events from a vantage point 25,000 light-years from the center of the Milky Way. However, we should mention that the Sun may not be at that distance throughout the sequence of events, as the collision readjusts the orbits of many stars within each galaxy. (credit: NASA; ESA; Z. Levay, R. van der Marel, STScl; T. Hallas, and A. Mellinger)

Chapter 15 Figure Pipeline: Streams in the Galactic Halo
1. Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 15.6.3 Streams in the Galactic Halo</h2>

<p>An illustration of the Milky Way with tidal stellar streams orbiting through its halo.</p>

<h3>Illustration Elements</h3>

<ul>
    <li>The Milky Way's spiral disk sits at the bottom of the image, tilted about 45 degrees from edge-on, with blue spiral arms and a bright yellow-white central bulge.</li>
    <li>A small companion galaxy, a faint bluish-white blob, appears near the top of the image, connected to one of the streams.</li>
    <li>Three streams of stars, each shown as a thin trail of small white points, trace looping elliptical orbits around the galactic center.</li>
    <li>Two of the streams loop close enough to the center that their stars pass through the plane of the disk.</li>
    <li>One stream forms a much larger loop that orbits the entire galaxy without touching the disk at all.</li>
    <li>Credit: modification of work by NASA/JPL-Caltech/R. Hurt (SSC/Caltech)</li>
</ul>
2. New Figure Caption Text
When a small galaxy is captured and torn apart by the Milky Way's gravity, its stars are stretched into long tidal streams in the halo, as illustrated here based on models of the Milky Way absorbing 50 dwarf galaxies over the past 10 billion years.

3. New Alt-Text
Tidal stellar streams orbiting the Milky Way's halo. (+ "Details in caption.")

4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
Original alt-text: Streams in the Galactic Halo. This illustration shows the Milky Way at bottom, with the disk tilted about 45 degrees from edge-on. Three streams of stars are shown tracing elliptical orbits around the galactic center. Two of the streams are close enough to the center to have their stars pass through the plane of the galaxy. One stream is so distant it orbits the entire galaxy and does not touch the disk at all.

Original caption text: Streams in the Galactic Halo. When a small galaxy is swallowed by the Milky Way, its member stars are stripped away and form streams of stars in the galactic halo. This image is based on calculations of what some of these tidal streams might look like if the Milky Way swallowed 50 dwarf galaxies over the past 10 billion years. (credit: modification of work by NASA/JPL-Caltech/R. Hurt (SSC/Caltech))

1. Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 15.6.2 Sagittarius Dwarf</h2>

<p>A greyscale image of the galactic center overlaid with a contour map of the Sagittarius Dwarf galaxy.</p>

<h3>Image Elements</h3>

<ul>
    <li>The background is a greyscale image of the Milky Way's disk, showing dense star fields and dark dust lanes.</li>
    <li>A red contour map, positioned above and slightly to the right of center, marks the brightness of the Sagittarius Dwarf galaxy, which lies on the far side of the galactic center.</li>
    <li>Small white star symbols within the red region mark the locations of several globular clusters belonging to the Sagittarius Dwarf galaxy.</li>
    <li>A cross near the center of the image marks the galactic center.</li>
    <li>A horizontal line running across the image marks the galactic plane.</li>
    <li>A blue outline tracing above and below the galactic plane corresponds to Herschel's diagram of the Milky Way.</li>
    <li>Several small yellow boxes are scattered across the image, marking regions where detailed studies of individual stars led to the discovery of this galaxy.</li>
    <li>Credit: modification of work by R. Ibata (UBC), R. Wyse (JHU), R. Sword (IoA)</li>
</ul>
2. New Figure Caption Text
Discovered in 1994, the Sagittarius Dwarf galaxy lies about 50,000 light-years from the Milky Way's center on the far side of the Galaxy and is gradually falling into it, as shown by this composite image combining a visible-light view of the disk with a contour map of the dwarf galaxy's brightness.

3. New Alt-Text
Sagittarius Dwarf galaxy contour map over Milky Way disk. (+ "Details in caption.")

4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
Original alt-text: Sagittarius Dwarf Galaxy. Superimposed on this greyscale image of the galactic center a contour map of the dwarf galaxy is drawn in red above and slightly to the right of center. The white stars in the contour map mark the locations of several globular clusters contained within the dwarf galaxy. The cross marks the center of the Milky Way. The horizontal line corresponds to the galactic plane. The blue outline on either side of the galactic plane corresponds to Herschel's diagram of the Milky Way. The boxes mark regions where detailed studies of individual stars led to the discovery of this galaxy.

Original caption text: Sagittarius Dwarf. In 1994, astronomers discovered a galaxy in the constellation of Sagittarius, located only about 50,000 light-years from the center of the Milky Way and falling into our Galaxy. This image covers a region approximately 70° × 50° and combines a black-and-white view of the disk of our Galaxy with a red contour map showing the brightness of the dwarf galaxy. The dwarf galaxy lies on the other side of the galactic center from us. The white stars in the red region mark the locations of several globular clusters contained within the Sagittarius dwarf galaxy. The cross marks the galactic center. The horizontal line corresponds to the galactic plane. The blue outline on either side of the galactic plane corresponds to the infrared image in Figure (\PageIndex{1}). The boxes mark regions where detailed studies of individual stars led to the discovery of this galaxy. (credit: modification of work by R. Ibata (UBC), R. Wyse (JHU), R. Sword (IoA))

Chapter 15 Figure Pipeline: Monolithic Collapse Model
1. Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 15.6.1 Monolithic Collapse Model</h2>

<p>A four-panel illustration of the monolithic collapse model for the formation of the Milky Way.</p>

<h3>Panel 1</h3>

<ul>
    <li>In the upper left</li>
    <li>A rotating gas cloud, drawn as a blue blob, at the start of its collapse.</li>
    <li>A vertical line above the cloud's center, with a counterclockwise arrow around it, marks the axis of rotation.</li>
    <li>White arrows around the edge of the cloud point inward toward the center, illustrating gravitational collapse.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Panel 2</h3>

<ul>
    <li>In the upper right</li>
    <li>The gas cloud has flattened somewhat at the edges and grown thicker near the axis of rotation.</li>
    <li>Small white dots outside the cloud are labeled Globular clusters.</li>
    <li>A few white arrows near the cloud's edge continue to point inward, indicating ongoing collapse.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Panel 3</h3>

<ul>
    <li>In the lower left</li>
    <li>The cloud has flattened further, continuing to collapse into a disk shape.</li>
    <li>White arrows point inward toward the disk from multiple directions above and below it.</li>
    <li>Globular clusters, shown as small white dots, are scattered above and below the flattening disk.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Panel 4</h3>

<ul>
    <li>In the lower right</li>
    <li>The galaxy has become a thin, bright white disk with a glowing central bulge, indicating that stars have now formed.</li>
    <li>Globular clusters, shown as small blue dots, are evenly distributed in a scattered pattern around the galactic bulge, above and below the disk.</li>
</ul>
2. New Figure Caption Text
In the monolithic collapse model, the Milky Way formed from a single rotating gas cloud that collapsed into a disk; halo stars and globular clusters formed early, before or during the collapse, while disk stars formed later from gas already enriched with heavy elements from earlier stellar generations.

3. New Alt-Text
Four-panel monolithic collapse model of Milky Way formation. (+ "Details in caption.")

4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
Original alt-text: Monolithic Collapse Model for the Formation of the Galaxy. Panel 1 at upper left shows the gas cloud, drawn as a blue blob, at the beginning of its collapse. The axis of rotation (drawn in all four panels) is a vertical line above center with a counter-clockwise arrow around it indicating the direction of rotation. White arrows at the periphery of the cloud point toward the center illustrating the collapse. Panel 2 at upper right shows the gas cloud flattened a bit at the edges and thicker nearer the axis of rotation. Globular clusters are indicated as white dots outside the cloud. Panel 3 at lower left shows the cloud further flattened and continuing to collapse into a disk. Finally, panel 4 at lower right shows the galaxy much thinner, and now drawn in white to indicate that stars have formed in the disk. Globular clusters are evenly distributed around the galactic bulge.

Original caption text: Monolithic Collapse Model. According to this model, the Milky Way Galaxy initially formed from a rotating cloud of gas that collapsed due to gravity. Halo stars and globular clusters either formed prior to the collapse or were formed elsewhere. Stars in the disk formed later, when the gas from which they were made was already "contaminated" with heavy elements produced in earlier generations of stars.

Chapter 15 Figure Pipeline: Andromeda Galaxy
Note: this figure carries a third-party credit (Adam Evans) rather than the standard Openstax author line, so per guidelines I've used the standard Openstax attribution in the figure HTML and appended the original credit to the end of the accessible description.

1. Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 15.5.2 Andromeda Galaxy</h2>

<p>A visible light image of the spiral galaxy Andromeda (M31), seen slightly tilted from edge-on.</p>

<h3>Image Elements</h3>

<ul>
    <li>A large, elongated spiral galaxy stretches diagonally across the image, tilted nearly edge-on.</li>
    <li>A bright, glowing yellowish-white central bulge sits at the middle of the galaxy, populated by older stars.</li>
    <li>Bluer, younger stars trace the outer spiral arms extending outward from the bulge.</li>
    <li>Dark brownish dust lanes run through the disk, partially blocking light from the central bulge.</li>
    <li>Two smaller, fuzzy companion galaxies appear near the bottom of the main galaxy, one closer and brighter, one fainter and more distant.</li>
    <li>The surrounding background is filled with countless foreground stars of the Milky Way, appearing as small white, blue, and orange points of light.</li>
    <li>Credit: Adam Evans</li>
</ul>
2. New Figure Caption Text
Andromeda (M31), the nearest large spiral galaxy to the Milky Way, shares our Galaxy's basic structure, with an older, yellowish central bulge, younger blue stars in the outer disk, and dust lanes that dim light from the bulge.

3. New Alt-Text
Andromeda Galaxy (M31), spiral arms and dust lanes. (+ "Details in caption.")

4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
Original alt-text: Visible Light Image of the Andromeda Galaxy (M31). The nearest large spiral galaxy to the Milky Way is slightly tilted from edge-on, allowing us to view the blue spiral arms as well as strong dust lanes that block some of the light from the central bulge.

Original caption text: Andromeda Galaxy. This neighboring spiral looks similar to our own Galaxy in that it is a disk galaxy with a central bulge. Note the bulge of older, yellowish stars in the center, the bluer and younger stars in the outer regions, and the dust in the disk that blocks some of the light from the bulge. (credit: Adam Evans)

Chapter 15 Figure Pipeline: Thin Disk vs. Halo Orbits
1. Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 15.5.1 Thin Disk vs. Halo Orbits</h2>

<p>Two diagrams comparing the orbital paths of stars in the Galaxy's thin disk and halo.</p>

<h3>Diagram (a): Thin Disk</h3>

<ul>
    <li>On the left, labeled Thin disk.</li>
    <li>A flat, translucent blue plane labeled Galactic plane extends diagonally across the image.</li>
    <li>Several concentric blue elliptical orbits lie flat within the plane, centered on a plus sign marking the galactic center.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Diagram (b): Halo</h3>

<ul>
    <li>On the right, labeled Halo.</li>
    <li>The same flat, translucent blue plane labeled Galactic plane extends diagonally across the image.</li>
    <li>Numerous blue elliptical orbits of varying sizes and orientations surround the plane, tilted at many different angles and extending well above and below it, all centered on a plus sign marking the galactic center.</li>
</ul>
2. New Figure Caption Text
Stars in the thin disk follow nearly circular orbits confined to the galactic plane, while stars in the halo move on randomly oriented, elliptical orbits that carry them far above and below it.

3. New Alt-Text
Thin disk circular orbits vs. halo's randomly tilted orbits. (+ "Details in caption.")

4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
Original alt-text: Orbital Motions in the Milky Way. In panel (a), at top and labeled Thin Disk, shows the orbits of stars as blue concentric ellipses centered on a + sign indicating the galactic center. The orbits are in the same plane, labeled Galactic plane. In panel (b), at bottom and labeled Halo, shows the orbits of stars as blue ellipses of many different sizes and orientations extending above and below the galactic plane and centered on a + sign indicating the galactic center.

Original caption text: Thin Disk vs. Halo Orbits. (a) In this image, you see stars in the thin disk of our Galaxy in nearly circular orbits. (b) In this image, you see the motion of stars in the Galaxy's halo in randomly oriented and elliptical orbits.

1. Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 15.4.4 Galactic Center in Near Infrared</h2>

<p>A near-infrared image of the galactic center with the measured orbits of eight stars plotted as ellipses.</p>

<h3>Image Elements</h3>

<ul>
    <li>The background is a near-infrared image, acquired with the Keck Telescope, showing scattered blue, green, yellow, and orange-red patches of light against a dark blue field.</li>
    <li>Eight colored ellipses of different colors, including cyan, red, magenta, orange, and green, are plotted over the image, each representing the orbit of a single star around the galactic center.</li>
    <li>Small dots of matching color lie along each ellipse, marking the individual observed data points along that star's orbit.</li>
    <li>All eight orbits converge around a common point near the center of the image, marking the location of the massive object they orbit.</li>
    <li>A scale bar in the upper left, shown as a short double-headed arrow, is labeled 0.1 arcseconds.</li>
    <li>An orientation indicator in the upper right shows North pointing up and East pointing left.</li>
    <li>Credit: modification of work by Andrea Ghez, UCLA Galactic Center Group, W.M. Keck Observatory Laser Team</li>
</ul>
2. New Figure Caption Text
Tracks of eight stars orbiting the galactic center, measured from 1995 to 2014 with the Keck Telescope, all point to a single massive object concentrated within this small region at the center of the Milky Way.

3. New Alt-Text
Keck infrared image with orbits of eight stars around galactic center. (+ "Details in caption.")

4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
Original alt-text: Near-infrared View of the Galactic Center. The measured orbits of eight stars are plotted orbiting the galactic center, shown as ellipses of different colors. The dots that lie along each ellipse are the observed data points. The scale at upper left, indicated with a short double headed arrow, reads: 0.1 arcseconds. At upper right the orientation of the image is indicated with arrows, north is up and east is to the left.

Original caption text: Galactic Center in Near Infrared. This image shows the inner 1 arcsecond, or 0.13 light-year, at the center of the Galaxy, as observed with the giant Keck Telescope. Tracks of the orbiting stars measured from 1995 to 2014 have been added to this "snapshot." The stars are moving around the center very fast, and their tracks are all consistent with a single massive "gravitator" that resides in the very center of this image. (credit: modification of work by Andrea Ghez, UCLA Galactic Center Group, W.M. Keck Observatory Laser Team)

Chapter 15 Figure Pipeline: Sagittarius A*
1. Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 15.4.3 Sagittarius A*</h2>

<p>A radio image of the central 10 light-years of the Milky Way, acquired by the Very Large Array.</p>

<h3>Image Elements</h3>

<ul>
    <li>A bright, compact white and red glowing region in the lower right marks Sagittarius A*, the galactic center.</li>
    <li>A set of long, straight, thread-like filaments, collectively known as The Arc, runs diagonally from the lower left to the upper right of the image.</li>
    <li>Fainter, wispy red filaments and streamers of ionized gas curve and drift across the upper portion of the image.</li>
    <li>Scattered small bright red points appear throughout the gas streamers, marking denser knots of emission.</li>
    <li>Credit: modification of work by Farhad Zadeh et al. (Northwestern), VLA, NRAO</li>
</ul>
2. New Figure Caption Text
This Very Large Array radio image maps hot, ionized gas near the Milky Way's center, showing streamers of gas and the filamentary structure known as The Arc converging near the bright source Sagittarius A*.

3. New Alt-Text
VLA radio image of Sagittarius A* and the Arc filaments. (+ "Details in caption.")

4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
Original alt-text: Central 10 Light-Years of the Galaxy. The bright region at lower right is Sagittarius A* and the straight filaments running from lower left to upper right are collectively known as The Arc.

Original caption text: Sagittarius A*. This image, taken with the Very Large Array of radio telescopes, shows the radio emission from hot, ionized gas in the center of the Milky Way. The lines slanting across the top of the image are gas streamers. Sagittarius A* is the bright spot in the lower right. (credit: modification of work by Farhad Zadeh et al. (Northwestern), VLA, NRAO)

Chapter 15 Figure Pipeline: Galactic Center in X-Rays
1. Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 15.4.2 Galactic Center in X-Rays</h2>

<p>A false-color X-ray image of the galactic center region, acquired by the Chandra X-ray Observatory.</p>

<h3>Image Elements</h3>

<ul>
    <li>An irregular white source at the center of the image marks Sagittarius A*, surrounded by diffuse orange and purple clouds of hot gas.</li>
    <li>Bright blue-green blobs to the left of center and at the far right mark supernova remnants.</li>
    <li>Countless small point sources of white, blue, and red light are scattered across the image, representing white dwarfs, neutron stars, and stellar black holes.</li>
    <li>The colors represent different X-ray energy bands: red for low energy, green for medium energy, and blue for high energy.</li>
    <li>Credit: modification of work by NASA/CXC/UMass/D. Wang et al.</li>
</ul>
2. New Figure Caption Text
This Chandra X-ray mosaic, centered on Sagittarius A*, reveals point sources such as white dwarfs, neutron stars, and stellar black holes, along with a diffuse haze of 10-million-K gas flowing outward from the galactic center.

3. New Alt-Text
Chandra X-ray mosaic of galactic center, Sagittarius A*. (+ "Details in caption.")

4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
Original alt-text: Galactic Center in X-rays. The galactic center is seen in this image as an irregular white source at center, surrounded by clouds of hot gas. Bright supernova remnants are seen to the left of center and at far right as blue-green blobs. The colors in this false-color image indicate X-ray energy bands: red (low energy), green (medium energy), and blue (high energy).

Original caption text: Galactic Center in X-Rays. This artificial-color mosaic of 30 images taken with the Chandra X-ray satellite shows a region 400 × 900 light-years in extent and centered on Sagittarius A*, the bright white source in the center of the picture. The X-ray-emitting point sources are white dwarfs, neutron stars, and stellar black holes. The diffuse "haze" is emission from gas at a temperature of 10 million K. This hot gas is flowing away from the center out into the rest of the Galaxy. The colors indicate X-ray energy bands: red (low energy), green (medium energy), and blue (high energy). (credit: modification of work by NASA/CXC/UMass/D. Wang et al.)

Chapter 15 Figure Pipeline: Galactic Center in Radio
1. Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 15.4.1 Galactic Center in Radio</h2>

<p>A radio image of the galactic center region, acquired at 90 centimeter wavelength by the Very Large Array (VLA), with many labeled features.</p>

<h3>Image Elements</h3>

<ul>
    <li>Against a dark reddish-brown background, bright orange and white patches and filaments trace the radio-bright structures near the galactic center.</li>
    <li>A very bright, compact white region near the center is labeled Sgr A*, marking the galactic center itself.</li>
    <li>A bright elongated feature just above and left of Sgr A* is labeled Arc.</li>
    <li>Several thin, thread-like filaments extend from the bright central region toward the upper right, labeled Threads, with additional nearby features labeled New SNR 0.3+0.0, New feature: The Cane, and Background galaxy.</li>
    <li>In the upper left, a chain of bright patches is labeled, from top to bottom, Sgr D HII, Sgr D SNR, SNR 0.9+0.1, Sgr B2, and Sgr B1.</li>
    <li>Below and right of Sgr A*, additional labeled features include New thread: The Pelican, Sgr C, and a cluster of faint filaments labeled Coherent structure?.</li>
    <li>Further down, a curved filament is labeled Snake, near a labeled region Sgr E.</li>
    <li>Near the bottom, two ring-shaped shell structures are labeled SNR 359.1-00.5 and Mouse, with SNR 359.0-00.9 labeled nearby.</li>
    <li>A scale bar in the lower left, shown as a double-headed horizontal arrow, is labeled approximately 0.5 degrees, approximately 75 pc, approximately 240 LY.</li>
    <li>Credit: modification of work by N. E. Kassim, D. S. Briggs, T. J. W. Lazio, T. N. LaRosa, and J. Imamura (NRL/RSD)</li>
</ul>
2. New Figure Caption Text
This Very Large Array radio map reveals the complex structure at the Milky Way's galactic center, including the Sagittarius A region, star-forming regions Sagittarius B1 and B2, thread-like filaments, and several supernova remnant shells.

3. New Alt-Text
VLA radio map of galactic center with labeled features. (+ "Details in caption.")

4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
Original alt-text: Radio Image of Galactic Center Region. Many features are identified in this complex radio image. The scale at lower left (defined by a double headed horizontal arrow) reads: approximately 0.5 degrees, approximately 75 pc, approximately 240 LY. The objects listed, from upper left to lower right, are: Sgr D HII, Sgr D SNR, SNR 0.9+0.1, Sgr B2, Sgr B1, New SNR 0.3+0.0, Arc, Threads, Sgr A*, New feature: The Cane, Background Galaxy, Threads, New thread: The Pelican, Sgr C, Coherent structure?, Snake and Sgr E. Below center, three more features are labeled (from top to bottom): SNR 359.1-00.5, Mouse and SNR 359.0-00.9.

Original caption text: Galactic Center in Radio. This radio map of the center of the Galaxy (at a wavelength of 90 centimeters) was constructed from data obtained with the Very Large Array (VLA) of radio telescopes in Socorro, New Mexico. Brighter regions are more intense in radio waves. The galactic center is inside the region labeled Sagittarius A. Sagittarius B1 and B2 are regions of active star formation. Many filaments or threadlike features are seen, as well as a number of shells (labeled SNR), which are supernova remnants. The scale bar at the bottom left is about 240 light-years long. Notice that radio astronomers also give fanciful animal names to some of the structures, much as visible-light nebulae are sometimes given the names of animals they resemble. (credit: modification of work by N. E. Kassim, D. S. Briggs, T. J. W. Lazio, T. N. LaRosa, and J. Imamura (NRL/RSD))
