Chapter 15 Figure Pipeline: Rotation Curve of the Galaxy
1. Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 15.3.1 Rotation Curve of the Galaxy</h2>

<p>A chart of the Milky Way's rotation curve compared to a predicted curve for enclosed mass.</p>

<h3>Chart Elements</h3>

<ul>
    <li>The vertical axis is labeled Orbital Velocity (km/s) and ranges from 0 at the bottom to 300 at the top, in increments of 100 km/s.</li>
    <li>The horizontal axis is labeled Distance from Center of Galaxy (kpc) and ranges from 0 at the left to 35 at the right, in increments of 5 kpc.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Observed Rotation Curve</h3>

<ul>
    <li>Drawn in red.</li>
    <li>Starts at the origin at the lower left and rises quickly to about 250 km/s at about 2 kpc.</li>
    <li>Drops to about 200 km/s at 5 kpc, then rises again to near 250 km/s at about 7.5 kpc.</li>
    <li>Drops slightly again to near 200 km/s at 10 kpc, then begins a slow, steady rise to almost 300 km/s at 35 kpc.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Predicted Curve for Enclosed Mass</h3>

<ul>
    <li>Drawn in blue.</li>
    <li>Shows what the rotation curve would look like if all matter in the Galaxy were located inside a radius of 10 kpc.</li>
    <li>Begins near 250 km/s at 15 kpc and steadily drops to about 100 km/s at 35 kpc.</li>
</ul>

<p>Key Trend: Instead of declining with distance as expected if most of the Galaxy's mass were concentrated near the center, the observed orbital speed of gas clouds stays high or even increases at large distances, indicating substantial unseen mass beyond the Sun's orbit.</p>
2. New Figure Caption Text
The observed rotation curve stays flat or rises with distance from the galactic center, rather than declining as the blue predicted curve would suggest if most of the Galaxy's mass were concentrated within the inner 10 kpc, providing evidence for dark matter extending well beyond the Sun's orbit.

3. New Alt-Text
Milky Way rotation curve vs. enclosed-mass prediction. (+ "Details in caption.")

4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
Original alt-text: In this plot the vertical axis is labeled Orbital Velocity (km/s), and ranges from zero at bottom to 300 at top, in increments of 100 km/s. The horizontal axis is labeled Distance from Center of Galaxy (kpc), and ranges from zero at left to 35 at right, in increments of 5 kpc. The rotation curve is drawn in red and starts at the origin at lower left, and rises quickly to about 250 km/s at about 2 kpc. The curve drops to about 200 km/s at 5 kpc, then rises again to near 250 km/s at about 7.5 kpc. From there is drops slightly again to near 200 km/s at 10 kpc, then begins a slow, steady rise to almost 300 km/s at 35 kpc. The blue curve shows what the rotation curve would look like if all of the matter in the Galaxy were located inside a radius of 10 kpc. The blue curve begins to drop from about 250 km/s at 15 kpc down to 100 km/s at 35 kpc.

Original caption text: Rotation Curve of the Galaxy. The orbital speed of carbon monoxide (CO) and hydrogen (H) gas at different distances from the center of the Milky Way Galaxy is shown in red. The blue curve shows what the rotation curve would look like if all the matter in the Galaxy were located inside a radius of 50,000 light-years. Instead of going down, the speed of gas clouds farther out remains high, indicating a great deal of mass beyond the Sun's orbit. The horizontal axis shows the distance from the galactic center in kiloparsecs (where a kiloparsec equals 3,260 light-years).

Chapter 15 Figure Pipeline: Differential Galactic Rotation
1. Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 15.2.3 Differential Galactic Rotation</h2>

<p>A four-panel illustration modeling how spiral arms could form from galactic rotation.</p>

<h3>Panel 1</h3>

<ul>
    <li>Two separate, irregular blue blobs of gas, one above the other, within a dark circle.</li>
    <li>A short curved arrow at the top points to the right, indicating the direction of rotation.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Panel 2</h3>

<ul>
    <li>The blobs have begun to stretch, with the inner portions moving further around the circle than the outer portions, giving each blob the appearance of a small comet.</li>
    <li>A longer curved arrow, covering roughly 90 degrees, indicates the rotation.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Panel 3</h3>

<ul>
    <li>The blobs are further curved and elongated, beginning to wrap around the center of the circle.</li>
    <li>The curved arrow now covers about 180 degrees.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Panel 4</h3>

<ul>
    <li>The elongated blobs have wound into a classic two-armed spiral shape filling most of the circle.</li>
    <li>The curved arrow now covers about 270 degrees.</li>
</ul>
2. New Figure Caption Text
Because regions farther from the galactic center take longer to complete an orbit than regions closer in, differential rotation can stretch irregular clouds of gas into spiral arms; since real spiral arms persist over time rather than winding up completely, other processes must also help maintain them.

3. New Alt-Text
Four-panel model of spiral arm formation by differential rotation. (+ "Details in caption.")

4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
Original alt-text: Simplified Model for the Formation of Spiral Arms. At left, the illustration begins with two irregular blue blobs, one above the other, with a short curved arrow at top pointing to the right indicating the direction of rotation. The next frame, with a longer curved arrow, shows how parts of the initial blobs have moved toward each other, but the parts further away have moved less, giving the appearance of two small comets. In the next frame, the curved arrow covers about 180°, and the blobs are now even more curved and elongated. In the final frame at right, the curved arrow covers 270°, and the classic spiral shape has emerged.

Original caption text: Differential Galactic Rotation. This sketch shows how spiral arms might form from irregular clouds of interstellar material stretched out by the different rotation rates throughout the Galaxy. The regions farthest from the galactic center take longer to complete their orbits and thus lag behind the inner regions. If this were the only mechanism for creating spiral arms, then over time the spiral arms would completely wind up and disappear. Since many galaxies have spiral arms, they must be long-lived, and there must be other processes at work to maintain them.

Chapter 15 Figure Pipeline: Orion Spur
1. Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 15.2.2 Orion Spur</h2>

<p>An illustration of the Milky Way's spiral arms, marking the Sun's location and nearby objects of interest.</p>

<h3>Illustration Elements</h3>

<ul>
    <li>Three spiral arms of the Milky Way run diagonally across the image, each labeled in yellow: the Cygnus arm at the top, the Perseus arm through the middle, and the Sagittarius arm at the bottom.</li>
    <li>The Orion Spur is a fainter stream of stars and gas that runs from the Cygnus arm diagonally down through the Perseus arm and into the Sagittarius arm, labeled in yellow near its midpoint.</li>
    <li>The Sun is marked with a white line, positioned along the Orion Spur between the Perseus and Sagittarius arms.</li>
    <li>White lines point from above the image to four labeled objects along the top: Sn2 289, Perseus Transit, Rosetta Nebula, and Orion Nebula, each appearing as a small reddish-pink patch within the spiral arms.</li>
    <li>White lines point from below the image to five labeled objects along the bottom, from left to right: Turner S, Vela Molecular Ridge, Gum Nebula, Cygnus X, and W51, each appearing as a small reddish-pink patch within the spiral arms.</li>
</ul>
2. New Figure Caption Text
The Sun lies within the Orion Spur, a minor spiral arm located between the Perseus and Sagittarius arms, alongside several other notable star-forming regions and objects.

3. New Alt-Text
Sun's location in the Orion Spur among Milky Way's spiral arms. (+ "Details in caption.")

4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
Original alt-text: The Sun and the Orion Spur. Portions of three spiral arms of the Milky Way are shown in this illustration. The Cygnus arm at top, the Perseus arm at center and the Sagittarius arm at bottom. The Orion spur is a stream of stars and gas runs from the Cygnus arm diagonally downward to the right through the Perseus arm and on to the Sagittarius arm. The Sun is located in the portion of the spur between the Perseus and Sagittarius arms. Objects of interest are indicated with arrows from above and below the figure. At top, from left to right are: Sn2 289, Perseus transit, Rosetta nebula and the Orion nebula. At bottom, from left to right are: Turner S, Vela molecular ridge, Gum nebula, Sun, Cygnus X-1 and W51.

Original caption text: Orion Spur. The Sun is located in the Orion Spur, which is a minor spiral arm located between two other arms. In this diagram, the white lines point to some other noteworthy objects that share this feature of the Milky Way Galaxy with the Sun. (credit: modification of work by NASA/JPL-Caltech)

Chapter 15 Figure Pipeline: Milky Way Bar and Arms
1. Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 15.2.1 Milky Way Bar and Arms</h2>

<p>A map of the Milky Way viewed from above, with a polar coordinate grid centered on the Sun.</p>

<h3>Grid Elements</h3>

<ul>
    <li>A polar coordinate system is centered on the Sun, located about halfway between the galactic center and the bottom of the image.</li>
    <li>Galactic Longitude is labeled around the outer edge, with 0 degrees straight up from the Sun, 90 degrees to the left, 180 degrees straight down, and 270 degrees to the right, marked in increments of 30 degrees.</li>
    <li>Concentric circles centered on the Sun mark distance from the Sun, labeled from 15,000 ly near the Sun to 75,000 ly near the outer edge, in increments of 15,000 ly.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Galaxy Structure</h3>

<ul>
    <li>A bright yellow-white central bulge sits at the middle of the image, elongated diagonally and labeled Galactic Bar.</li>
    <li>Two short segments of the bar nearest the center are labeled Near 3kpc Arm and Far 3kpc Arm, with a longer segment labeled Long Bar extending further along the same line.</li>
    <li>Spiral arms, shown as light blue bands dotted with reddish-pink patches of star formation, wind outward from the bar and bulge across the rest of the disk.</li>
    <li>Along the 0 degree line, moving outward from the Sun, the Sagittarius Arm is labeled.</li>
    <li>Along the 330 degree line, to the right of zero, moving outward, the Norma Arm and then the Scutum-Centaurus Arm are labeled.</li>
    <li>Along the 90 degree line, moving outward, the Orion Spur, Perseus Arm, and Outer Arm are labeled in sequence, with the Sun located at the inner end of the Orion Spur.</li>
</ul>
2. New Figure Caption Text
This top-down map, assembled from Spitzer Space Telescope infrared data, shows the Milky Way's modest central bar with the Scutum-Centaurus and Perseus arms winding out from its ends, while the fainter Sagittarius and Outer arms contain comparatively fewer stars.

3. New Alt-Text
Milky Way map with bar, spiral arms, and Sun's location. (+ "Details in caption.")

4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
Original alt-text: Map of the The Milky Way Galaxy. Over-plotted on this data-based illustration of the Milky Way is a coordinate system centered on the Sun, which is located about half way from the center and the bottom of the image. It is a polar coordinate system, with zero degrees straight up from the Sun, 90° to the left, 180° straight down and 270° to the right. Distances are shown as circles of increasing radius centered on the Sun. Distances from 15,000 ly to 75,000 ly are indicated in increments of 5,000 ly. Moving outward from the Sun along the zero degree line are the Near 3kpc Arm, Far 3 kpc Arm and the Sagittarius Arm. Moving outward from the Sun along the 330° line (to the right of zero) are the Norma Arm and the Scutum-Centaurus Arm. Moving outward from the Sun along the 90° line are the: Orion Spur, Perseus Arm and the Outer Arm.

Original caption text: Milky Way Bar and Arms. Here, we see the Milky Way Galaxy as it would look from above. This image, assembled from data from NASA's Spitzer mission, shows that the Milky Way Galaxy has a modest bar in its central regions. Two spiral arms, Scutum-Centaurus and Perseus, emerge from the ends of the bar and wrap around the bulge. The Sagittarius and Outer arms have fewer stars than the other two arms. (credit: modification of work by NASA/JPL-Caltech/R. Hurt (SSC/Caltech))

Chapter 15 Figure Pipeline: Galactic Center
1. Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 15.1.5 Galactic Center</h2>

<p>An infrared image of the central bulge and thin disk of the Milky Way, acquired by 2MASS.</p>

<h3>Image Elements</h3>

<ul>
    <li>A wide, thin horizontal band of stars stretches across the full width of the image, brightest and widest near the center.</li>
    <li>The central region glows brightly white, showing the dense bulge of old stars surrounding the galactic center.</li>
    <li>Thin patches of orange and brown dust trace along the band on either side of the bright central bulge.</li>
    <li>Countless small white and blue points of light are scattered above and below the band, representing individual stars.</li>
    <li>The band narrows and dims toward both the left and right edges of the image, showing how thin and flat the disk is compared to its width.</li>
    <li>Credit: modification of work by 2MASS/J. Carpenter, T. H. Jarrett, and R. Hurt</li>
</ul>
2. New Figure Caption Text
Because interstellar dust absorbs infrared light much less than visible light, this Two Micron All Sky Survey (2MASS) infrared image reveals the Milky Way's central bulge of old stars and its thin disk, both normally hidden from view.

3. New Alt-Text
Infrared 2MASS image of Milky Way's bulge and thin disk. (+ "Details in caption.")

4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
Original alt-text: Infrared Image of the Inner Part of the Milky Way Galaxy. This 2MASS image, centered on the central bulge, perfectly illustrates how thin and flat is the disk of our galaxy.

Original caption text: Galactic Center. This beautiful infrared map, showing half a billion stars, was obtained as part of the Two Micron All Sky Survey (2MASS). Because interstellar dust does not absorb infrared as strongly as visible light, this view reveals the previously hidden bulge of old stars that surrounds the center of our Galaxy, along with the Galaxy's thin disk component. (credit: modification of work by 2MASS/J. Carpenter, T. H. Jarrett, and R. Hurt)

<h2>Figure 15.1.4 Components of the Milky Way</h2>

Chapter 15 Figure Pipeline: Unbarred and Barred Spiral Galaxies
1. Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 15.1.3 Unbarred and Barred Spiral Galaxies</h2>

<p>Two images comparing an unbarred spiral galaxy and a barred spiral galaxy.</p>

<h3>Image (a)</h3>

<ul>
    <li>On the left</li>
    <li>An image of the spiral galaxy M74, acquired in visible light.</li>
    <li>The galaxy is nearly symmetric, with tightly wound blue spiral arms curving smoothly into a small, bright, whitish nucleus at the center.</li>
    <li>Dark dust lanes trace along the inner edges of the spiral arms.</li>
    <li>Reddish-pink patches are scattered throughout the arms, marking regions of glowing gas.</li>
    <li>Credit: modification of work by ESO/PESSTO/S. Smartt</li>
</ul>

<h3>Image (b)</h3>

<ul>
    <li>On the right</li>
    <li>An image of the barred spiral galaxy NGC 1365, acquired in visible light.</li>
    <li>A yellowish bar of stars extends outward from both sides of the nucleus at the center.</li>
    <li>Two main spiral arms project from the ends of the bar, curving outward and populated with blue stars and reddish patches of glowing gas.</li>
    <li>Dark dust lanes trace along the bar and inner edges of the spiral arms.</li>
    <li>Credit: modification of work by ESO</li>
</ul>
2. New Figure Caption Text
The unbarred spiral M74 and the barred spiral NGC 1365 illustrate two ends of a structural range; the Milky Way's own barred spiral structure is thought to fall between the two.

3. New Alt-Text
Unbarred spiral M74 and barred spiral NGC 1365 by ESO. (+ "Details in caption.")

4. Original Alt-Text and Caption (preserved)
Original alt-text: Unbarred and Barred Spiral Galaxies. Panel (a), at left, shows the beautifully symmetric spiral form of M74. The blue spiral arms and dust lanes spiral neatly into the bright nucleus at center. Panel (b), at right, shows the barred spiral NGC 1365. A bar of yellow stars projects out from the nucleus at center, with a nearly straight blue arm at each end of the bar.

Original caption text: Unbarred and Barred Spiral Galaxies. (a) This image shows the unbarred spiral galaxy M74. It contains a small central bulge of mostly old yellow-red stars, along with spiral arms that are highlighted with the blue light from young hot stars. (b) This image shows the strongly barred spiral galaxy NGC 1365. The bulge and the fainter bar both appear yellowish because the brightest stars in them are mostly old yellow and red giants. Two main spiral arms project from the ends of the bar. As in M74, these spiral arms are populated with blue stars and red patches of glowing gas—hallmarks of recent star formation. The Milky Way Galaxy is thought to have a barred spiral structure that is intermediate between these two examples. (credit a: modification of work by ESO/PESSTO/S. Smartt; credit b: modification of work by ESO)

<h2>Figure 15.1.2 Diagram of the Galaxy</h2>

<p>Two schematic illustrations comparing the face-on and edge-on views of the Milky Way's spiral disk.</p>

<h3>Illustration (a): Face-on View</h3>

<ul>
    <li>On the left</li>
    <li>A blue spiral disk viewed from above, with a bright yellow-white Nuclear bulge at the center.</li>
    <li>A Central bar extends through the nuclear bulge, pointing toward the upper left.</li>
    <li>Four spiral arms wind outward from the center, each labeled:
    <ul>
        <li>Cygnus, the outermost arm, curving toward the lower left.</li>
        <li>Perseus, the next arm in, labeled along the bottom.</li>
        <li>Orion, a shorter spur labeled just above and inside the Perseus arm.</li>
        <li>An unlabeled arm curves toward the right side of the disk.</li>
    </ul>
    </li>
    <li>The Sun is marked with a line pointing to a position on the inner edge of the Orion spur.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Illustration (b): Edge-on View</h3>

<ul>
    <li>On the right</li>
    <li>The spiral disk viewed edge-on, appearing as a thin, bright vertical band with a bright yellow-white Nuclear bulge swelling at its center.</li>
    <li>The label Thin layer of dust identifies the narrow vertical band itself.</li>
    <li>Two horizontal arrows near the top mark the thickness of the band at different points: 2000 LY near the top of the bulge, and 400 LY within the thinner disk just below it.</li>
    <li>Scattered white dots surrounding the thin disk are labeled Globular clusters.</li>
    <li>A vertical double-headed arrow on the right spans the full height of the disk, labeled 26,000 LY.</li>
    <li>The Sun is marked with a line pointing to a position on the disk, level with the Sun's position in Illustration (a).</li>
</ul>

<h2>Figure 15.1.1 Herschel's Diagram of the Milky Way</h2>

<p>An illustration of Herschel's star-count model of the Milky Way's shape.</p>

<h3>Illustration Elements</h3>

<ul>
    <li>The overall shape is a wide, irregular outline extending horizontally across the image, thicker through the middle and tapering toward both ends.</li>
    <li>At the left end, the shape splits into two narrow branches, one extending toward the upper left and one toward the lower left.</li>
    <li>Small white dots represent individual stars scattered throughout the shape, with larger and more densely packed dots near the center and fewer, smaller dots near the edges and along the two branches.</li>
    <li>A single yellow dot labeled Sun is placed right of center, within the densest region of stars.</li>
    <li>The area surrounding the star-filled shape is plain dark blue, representing space beyond the region where Herschel counted stars.</li>
</ul>

IDEA Framework Highlights and Recommended Changes
Paragraph Text — IDEA Assessment
This section is a strong example of IDEA-aligned content. Per the IDEA Framework (Section 3.0), it actively counteracts the tendency for academic content to reflect "a mono-cultural, Western, 'upper' Caste, white, male, straight and binary version of human experience" by centering Indigenous and non-Western astronomical knowledge traditions. Per Section 7.4, it references scholars (Urton, Salomon, Gullberg) whose work elevates non-Western knowledge systems. No paragraph text was flagged in red.

Recommended Changes for Non-Text Material and Content
Figure XXX (Incan Dark Constellations): The figure is well-credited with a CC BY 4.0 license and attribution to Jessica Gullberg via SciEngine. This is a strong example of equitable attribution. No changes needed.

Reference to "the devil" — The sentence "Toads were thought of as bad omens as they were created by the devil" uses a term ("the devil") that is specific to Christian cosmology. Per the IDEA Framework (Section 2.0), it is important to "ensure examples do not presume knowledge that might be culture dependent." Since this is presented as a description of Inca belief as recorded by a Spanish chronicler (Polo de Ondegardo), consider adding a brief clarifying note that this framing reflects the Spanish colonial chronicler's interpretation rather than original Inca cosmological language. This would provide important socio-cultural context, consistent with IDEA Framework Section 2.0 guidance on providing context for material relevant to the experiences of marginalized communities.

Colonial framing of sources — The section relies heavily on Urton (1981) and other 20th-century Western academic sources to describe Inca knowledge traditions. Per the IDEA Framework (Section 7.4), consider supplementing these references with sources authored by scholars from Andean or Indigenous Quechua backgrounds, where available, to ensure that the knowledge traditions are represented through multiple lenses, including those of community members themselves.

Introduction paragraph — The introduction mentions seven cultures but focuses only on the Inca. Per the IDEA Framework (Section 7.4), the brief mention of the other six traditions (Maya, Moqoit, Tupi, Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islanders, Bugis, Xhosa/Sotho/Zulu/Tswana, Polynesians) without any elaboration could inadvertently suggest these traditions are less important. Consider either expanding the section to include at least brief descriptions of the other traditions, or adding a note that explicitly validates all seven traditions as equally rich and significant, and directing students to the full Gullberg (2020) article.

"time immemorial" — The phrase "viewed by humans since time immemorial" in the final paragraph is a positive acknowledgment of the deep history of dark constellation observation. However, per the IDEA Framework (Section 3.0), consider replacing "time immemorial" with a more specific acknowledgment, such as "for tens of thousands of years by Indigenous peoples across the Southern Hemisphere and beyond," to give more concrete recognition to the communities who developed these knowledge traditions.

General — Spelling note — "Brasil" in the second paragraph appears to be the Portuguese spelling of Brazil. This has been highlighted below. Per workflow step 11, spelling errors are highlighted in orange but not corrected.

Brasil

Please verify whether the Portuguese spelling is intentional (e.g., to honor the local name) or a typographical error.

IDEA Framework — Red highlight applied in figure caption — The phrase "British astronomers" in the Sagittarius Dwarf figure caption has been highlighted in red. Per the IDEA Framework Section 3.0, "What is claimed as 'history'...is actually a mono-cultural, Western, 'upper' Caste, white, male, straight and binary version of human experience." Identifying the discoverers only by national identity (British) without naming them or acknowledging the broader international community that contributed to this discovery reflects a Western-centric framing. Per Section 7.4, counteracting bias means "actively seeking out achievements and discipline contributions from all cultures and countries." The caption credit does include the researchers' names (R. Ibata, R. Wyse, R. Sword), but the paragraph text reduces them to a national identity. No changes were made to the text itself.
IDEA Framework Highlights and Recommended Changes
Red-Highlighted Text — Reasoning
"British astronomers" in the Sagittarius Dwarf figure caption: Per the IDEA Framework (Section 3.0 and Section 7.4), describing scientists solely by national identity without naming them, or without acknowledging contributions from researchers of diverse backgrounds, reflects a Western-centric framing of scientific discovery. The credit line includes three researchers (R. Ibata, R. Wyse, R. Sword) from UBC and JHU — institutions in Canada and the United States, not solely British. The label "British astronomers" is also potentially inaccurate. The text should be reviewed and corrected to name the researchers directly.
Recommended Changes for Non-Text Material (IDEA Framework)
Figure XXX (Monolithic Collapse Model): No credit is given for this illustration. Consider adding a credit line to recognize the creator, consistent with equitable attribution practices per the IDEA Framework (Section 7.4).

Figure XXX (Sagittarius Dwarf): The caption identifies researchers only by initials and institutional abbreviations (UBC, JHU, IoA). Consider expanding these to full names and full institutional names to provide complete and equitable attribution. Additionally, verify the nationality claim "British astronomers" in the paragraph text, as the credit line suggests affiliations with Canadian and American institutions.

Figure XXX (Streams in the Galactic Halo): The credit lists "R. Hurt (SSC/Caltech)" — a single researcher at a U.S. institution. Consider whether additional contributors to this visualization can be credited.

Figure XXX (Collision of the Milky Way with Andromeda): The credit lists five contributors (Z. Levay, R. van der Marel, T. Hallas, A. Mellinger). Consider expanding initials to full first names where possible to provide fuller recognition.

General — Scientific contributors: This section discusses galaxy formation models (monolithic collapse, multiple merger) without naming any of the scientists who developed these models. Per the IDEA Framework (Section 7.4), actively seeking out and naming contributors from diverse backgrounds — including non-Western scientists who have contributed to galactic formation theory — would strengthen the IDEA alignment of this section. For example, contributions from researchers such as Puragra Guhathakurta (UC Santa Cruz) on tidal streams, or work by researchers from institutions in India, China, Chile, and other countries on galactic structure, could be acknowledged.

General — Indigenous astronomical knowledge: The section discusses the Sagittarius dwarf galaxy and the Small Magellanic Cloud (referenced in the previous section) as objects visible or relevant to observers in the Southern Hemisphere. Per the IDEA Framework (Section 3.0), consider acknowledging that Indigenous peoples of the Southern Hemisphere — including Aboriginal Australians, Māori, and Andean peoples — have observed and named these objects for thousands of years, contributing to humanity's astronomical knowledge long before modern telescopes were built.

General — Language: The phrase "cannibalized dwarf galaxies" is informal and could be considered culturally insensitive given the history of the word "cannibalized" in colonial contexts. Per the IDEA Framework (Section 2.0 and 3.0), consider replacing it with a more neutral term such as "absorbed dwarf galaxies" or "accreted dwarf galaxies."

Recommended Section Headers
<h2>Building Our Galaxy</h2>
  [Introductory paragraph: Overview of how stellar population data provides clues
  to galaxy formation, and the two main models — monolithic collapse and
  multiple mergers.]

  <h3>The Protogalactic Cloud Model</h3>
    [Description of the monolithic collapse model; formation of the halo, disk,
    and globular clusters from a single rotating cloud; timescale of collapse.]

  <h3>Galaxy Mergers and Tidal Streams</h3>
    [Discovery of the Sagittarius dwarf galaxy; gravitational tidal stripping;
    identification of stellar streams in the halo; globular clusters as
    remnant nuclei of absorbed galaxies.]

  <h3>How Mergers Shape the Galaxy</h3>
    [Role of mergers in forming the thick disk; the Canis Major dwarf galaxy;
    the future collision with the Andromeda galaxy; environmental influences
    on galaxy evolution.]

IDEA Framework Highlights and Recommended Changes
Red-Highlighted Text — Reasoning
The passage about Walter Baade focuses solely on a white, Western, male scientist and his personal wartime circumstances as the origin story of the population I/II classification. Per the IDEA Framework (Section 7.4), textbook content should actively seek out and include contributions from scientists of all cultures and backgrounds. The current framing presents the history of this discovery as a single-person, single-culture narrative without acknowledging the broader international and diverse scientific community that has contributed to our understanding of stellar populations.

Recommended Changes for Non-Text Material
Figure XXX (Andromeda Galaxy, credit: Adam Evans): The credit is minimal. Consider verifying whether Adam Evans is the photographer and providing a more complete attribution, including any institutional affiliation, to model full and equitable recognition of contributors.
Figure XXX (Orbital Motions in the Milky Way): No credit is given for this figure. Consider adding a credit line to recognize the creator of this illustration.
General — Scientist representation: The section "Two Kinds of Stars" names only Walter Baade as the discoverer of the population classification system. Per the IDEA Framework (Section 7.4), consider adding context about other scientists — including those from non-Western or underrepresented backgrounds — who contributed to or built upon this work. For example, astronomers such as Cecilia Payne-Gaposchkin made foundational contributions to understanding stellar composition that underpins this classification.
General — Concept naming: Per the IDEA Framework (Section 7.4, STEM guidance), consider whether "Population I" and "Population II" need to be attributed to Baade by name in every instance, or whether the focus could shift more toward the conceptual meaning of the classification (e.g., "metal-rich disk stars" and "metal-poor halo stars") to reduce the centering of a single scientist's name.
General — Geographic representation: The Small Magellanic Cloud is described as "visible from Earth's Southern Hemisphere." Consider adding that this galaxy has been studied and observed by Indigenous peoples of the Southern Hemisphere for thousands of years, including Aboriginal Australians and Māori peoples, which would acknowledge non-Western astronomical knowledge traditions consistent with IDEA principles.
Recommended Section Headers
<h2>Stellar Populations in the Milky Way</h2>
  [Introductory paragraph: Overview of the 100–400 billion stars in the Milky Way,
  the concept of stellar populations, and what they reveal about the Galaxy's history.]

    [Description of thin disk, thick disk, and stellar halo; orbital patterns;
    relationship between location, age, and metal abundance.]

    including location, age, orbital motion, and chemical composition.]

  <h3>Heavy Elements and Stellar Generations</h3>
    [How heavy elements are created and recycled through stellar generations;
    why Population I stars are metal-rich and Population II stars are metal-poor.]

  <h3>Beyond Two Categories</h3>

    and central bulge; comparison with the Small Magellanic Cloud.]

General — Language: The repeated use of "monster" to describe the black hole (e.g., "monster black hole," "the monster in the center of the Galaxy") is informal and colloquial. While not an IDEA violation, consider whether this language is consistent with the tone expected in an OER textbook.

6. Recommended Changes (Including IDEA Standards)
Figures — Representation (IDEA 7.1): The one figure in this section is a data plot (rotation curve graph) with no people depicted. Per the IDEA Framework (7.1), more than 70% of photos and illustrations should include BIPOC individuals. Consider adding a contextual image of astronomers or researchers from diverse backgrounds who have contributed to dark matter research, such as Vera Rubin or researchers from international institutions.
Diverse Researchers (IDEA 7.4): The text discusses the discovery of dark matter and flat rotation curves without crediting the astronomers who made these discoveries. Vera Rubin and Kent Ford, who produced key observational evidence for dark matter in the 1970s, are not mentioned. Consider adding a sentence or sidebar crediting their contributions. Vera Rubin in particular is a significant figure whose work is directly relevant to this section.
Named Laws (IDEA 7.4): Per the IDEA Framework, referencing theory and concepts by a scientist's name elevates the person over the concept. "Kepler's third law" appears multiple times. The recommended replacement "the orbital period law" has been applied in the formatted HTML above. The section header should also be updated accordingly.
Newton reference (IDEA 7.4): The text references "Newton showed that..." Consider shifting the focus to the concept itself, e.g., "It can be shown that if you have matter distributed in the shape of a sphere..."
Figure alt text: The alt text for the rotation curve figure is detailed and descriptive, which is excellent for accessibility. However, the alt text references "a radius of 10 kpc" while the figure caption references "a radius of 50,000 light-years." These may be inconsistent and should be reviewed for accuracy.
Cross-references to other chapters: The text references "A Universe of (Mostly) Dark Matter and Dark Energy" and "Black Holes and Curved Spacetime" without hyperlinks. Consider adding links to these sections for navigation and accessibility.
CD analogy (from previous section): In the Formation of Spiral Structure section (previous HTML), the text uses "a CD inside your player" as an analogy. This cultural reference may not be universally familiar to all students, per the IDEA Framework guidance on avoiding assumptions about prior cultural knowledge. Consider replacing with a more universally accessible analogy, such as a spinning disk or a merry-go-round.

Alt-text
Diagram of gravitational microlensing by a black hole, showing views from Earth before, during, and after.
Figure caption text
As a black hole passes in front of a background star, its gravity bends and focuses the star's light, temporarily brightening and distorting the star's image as seen from Earth.

Accessible description
<h2>Figure 14.4.2 Gravitational Microlensing</h2>
<p>A diagram of a black hole causing gravitational microlensing of a background star's light.</p>

<h3>Overview</h3>
<ul>
    <li>Three side-by-side panels show the same star-black hole-Earth system at three points in time: before, during, and after a microlensing event.</li>
    <li>Each panel includes a star at the top, a black hole below it, an Earth icon below that, and a square inset showing what a ground-based observatory would see.</li>
    <li>A small illustration of a satellite and a ground-based observatory appear at the bottom of the figure, beneath the during panel.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Panel: Before</h3>
<ul>
    <li>On the left</li>
    <li>A white star is labeled Star at the top of the panel.</li>
    <li>Five yellow arrows point outward and downward from the star, representing light traveling away from it in multiple directions.</li>
    <li>An orange ring labeled Black hole sits below the star, off to one side, with a small blue arrow indicating it is moving to the right.</li>
    <li>A small Earth icon labeled Earth [before] appears below the black hole.</li>
    <li>A square inset labeled Star shows a single steady point of orange light, representing the star as normally seen from Earth.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Panel: During</h3>
<ul>
    <li>In the middle</li>
    <li>A star labeled Star sits at the top, with the label Light from star pointing to the rays traveling from it.</li>
    <li>The black hole, labeled Black hole with a blue arrow showing its motion to the right, has moved directly beneath the star.</li>
    <li>The light rays from the star bend around the black hole along curved yellow paths labeled Gravitational lensing before converging toward Earth.</li>
    <li>An Earth icon labeled Earth [during] sits below the black hole, directly in line with the bent light paths.</li>
    <li>A square inset labeled Microlensed image shows a bright, radiating point of white and orange light, much brighter than the point shown in the before panel, representing the brightened and distorted appearance of the star during the lensing event.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Panel: After</h3>
<ul>
    <li>On the right</li>
    <li>The layout matches the before panel: a star labeled Star at the top, five yellow arrows showing light traveling outward, a black hole labeled Black hole that has now moved past and beyond the star's light path, with a blue arrow showing continued motion to the right.</li>
    <li>An Earth icon labeled Earth [after] sits below the black hole.</li>
    <li>A square inset labeled Star shows a single steady point of orange light, the same as in the before panel, indicating the star has returned to its normal brightness.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Observatory Illustration</h3>
<ul>
    <li>Below the during panel, a small satellite illustration is shown to the left and a ground-based telescope dome is shown to the right, both labeled together as Ground-based observatory.</li>
</ul>

<p>Credit: NASA/ESA via ESAHubble.</p>

Here's the full set of components for Figure 14.4.1, built from the metadata you submitted and the composite image itself.

Alt-text
M87 black hole system in multiple wavelengths, from radio to gamma rays, using nineteen telescopes and observatories.
Figure caption text
This composite of images from nineteen telescopes and observatories shows the black hole at the center of the M87 galaxy and its jet across the entire electromagnetic spectrum, from radio to gamma-ray wavelengths. The images reveal how dramatically the angular resolution needed to see the event horizon differs from the resolution needed to trace the jet across the galaxy.

Accessible description
<h2>Figure 14.4.1 Black Hole M87</h2>
<p>Composite image of the M87 black hole across wavelengths.</p>

<h3>Overview</h3>
<ul>
    <li>Thirteen images of the M87 galaxy's central black hole and jet, each taken by a different telescope or combination of telescopes, are arranged to show how the view changes at increasing photon energy, from radio waves to gamma rays.</li>
    <li>The images are grouped in three columns: radio and millimeter images on the left, ultraviolet and visible images in the middle, and X-ray and gamma-ray images on the right.</li>
    <li>Within each column, a white box on one image marks the region shown in the next, more zoomed-in image.</li>
    <li>A bright knot in the jet, labeled Knot A, is marked with an arrow in several of the images.</li>
    <li>A horizontal electromagnetic spectrum scale at the bottom of the figure shows where each telescope's wavelength falls, from radio waves to cosmic rays.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Radio and Millimeter Images</h3>
<ul>
    <li>Six images, shown in orange and yellow, trace the jet from a wide view down to the black hole itself.</li>
    <li>The panel labeled ALMA 1.3 mm covers 5 arcseconds, or 1320 light-years, and shows a diffuse, elongated jet extending diagonally from a bright compact core at the lower left up toward the knot labeled Knot A at the upper right.</li>
    <li>The panel labeled EVN 170 mm covers 100 milliarcseconds, or 26.4 light-years, and zooms into the compact core seen in the ALMA image, appearing as a small bright orange patch.</li>
    <li>The panel labeled EAVN 13 mm covers 10 milliarcseconds, or 2.6 light-years, and shows the core as a larger, brighter patch with a short extension beginning to emerge toward the upper right.</li>
    <li>The panel labeled VLBA 7 mm covers 5 milliarcseconds, or 1.3 light-years, and shows the core as a bright, elongated oval with a faint extension of jet structure.</li>
    <li>The panel labeled GMVA 3.5 mm covers 1 milliarcsecond, or 0.26 light-years, and shows the core as a bright oval with faint striped structure suggesting the base of the jet.</li>
    <li>The panel labeled EHT 1.3 mm covers 50 microarcseconds, or 0.013 light-years, and shows the iconic ring-shaped image of the black hole: a bright orange ring, brighter along its lower edge, surrounding a dark central shadow cast by the event horizon.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Ultraviolet and Visible Images</h3>
<ul>
    <li>Three images, shown in blue, cover progressively smaller fields of view of the galaxy's core and jet.</li>
    <li>The panel labeled Swift 251 nm covers 10 arcseconds, or 2640 light-years, and shows two separate glowing blue regions: the galaxy's core near the bottom and the knot labeled Knot A near the top.</li>
    <li>The panel labeled Swift 439 nm covers the same field of view and shows the same two glowing regions, with a box marking the area around the core shown in the next image.</li>
    <li>The panel labeled HST 588 nm covers 5 arcseconds, or 1320 light-years, and shows a large glowing blue sphere representing the galaxy's central bulge, dotted with faint white points representing individual stars, with a thin blue jet extending diagonally from the bright core toward the upper right.</li>
</ul>

<h3>X-ray and Gamma-ray Images</h3>
<ul>
    <li>Four images show the jet and core at progressively lower resolution and higher photon energy, connected by thin white lines indicating their relative fields of view.</li>
    <li>The panel labeled Chandra 0.2-10 keV covers 5 arcseconds, or 1320 light-years, and shows the jet in blue, red, and yellow, with a bright yellow-white core at the left connected by a red and blue diffuse jet to a red knot at the upper right, labeled Knot A.</li>
    <li>The panel labeled NuSTAR 3-79 keV covers 10 arcminutes, or 160 light-years, and shows the source as a small, unresolved blob with concentric rings of purple, blue, green, and yellow.</li>
    <li>The panel labeled HESS+MAGIC+VERITAS 100 GeV-10 TeV covers 0.5 degrees, or 475 light-years, and shows the source as a small, unresolved green and yellow blob.</li>
    <li>The panel labeled Fermi-LAT 3-1000 GeV covers 2 degrees, or 1900 light-years, and shows the source as a small, unresolved, pixelated blob in pink, purple, and green.</li>
</ul>

<h3>Electromagnetic Spectrum Scale</h3>
<ul>
    <li>A horizontal bar diagram across the bottom of the figure represents the electromagnetic spectrum.</li>
    <li>The top axis is labeled wavelength in meters and ranges from 10<sup>2</sup> on the left to 10<sup>-16</sup> on the right, decreasing logarithmically by factors of 10.</li>
    <li>The bottom axis is labeled frequency in hertz and ranges from 10<sup>6</sup> on the left to 10<sup>24</sup> on the right, increasing logarithmically by factors of 10.</li>
    <li>The bar is divided into labeled bands, from left to right: Radio Waves, Microwaves, Infra-red, Visible, Ultraviolet, X-rays, gamma-rays, and Cosmic rays.</li>
    <li>Small triangular markers above the bar show where each telescope's wavelength falls, in order from left to right: EAVN 13 mm, VLBA 7 mm, GMVA 3.5 mm, EVN 170 mm, EHT and ALMA 1.3 mm, HST 578 nm, Swift 430 nm, Swift 251 nm, Chandra X-rays, NuSTAR, Fermi-LAT, and HESS+MAGIC+VERITAS.</li>
</ul>

<p>Credit: The EHT Multi-wavelength Science Working Group; the EHT Collaboration; ALMA (ESO/NAOJ/NRAO); the EVN; the EAVN Collaboration; VLBA (NRAO); the GMVA; the Hubble Space Telescope; the Neil Gehrels Swift Observatory; the Chandra X-ray Observatory; the Nuclear Spectroscopic Telescope Array; the Fermi-LAT Collaboration; the H.E.S.S collaboration; the MAGIC collaboration; the VERITAS collaboration; NASA and ESA. Composition by J. C. Algaba.</p>

Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 14.3.3 Black Hole Mergers</h2>
<p>A scatter chart comparing the masses of known black holes and neutron stars.</p>
<h3>Chart Elements</h3>
<ul>
    <li>The chart is titled Masses in the Stellar Graveyard.</li>
    <li>The vertical axis is labeled Solar Masses and is logarithmic, with gridlines at 1, 2, 5, 10, 20, 40, 80, and 160.</li>
    <li>The horizontal axis has no scale or label. Each position along it represents a separate observed black hole or neutron star, or merger event, rather than a measured quantity.</li>
    <li>Data points are grouped into four categories, from highest mass to lowest.</li>
</ul>
<h3>Data Categories</h3>
<ul>
    <li>LIGO-Virgo Black Holes
    <ul>
        <li>Shown as light blue dots across the top of the chart, ranging from about 20 to over 80 solar masses.</li>
        <li>Most points are grouped in pairs connected by a curved arrow leading downward to a third dot, representing two individual black holes merging into a single, more massive black hole.</li>
        <li>Several of the merged black holes near the center and right of the chart have masses above 80 solar masses, approaching or exceeding 160.</li>
    </ul>
    </li>
    <li>EM Black Holes
    <ul>
        <li>Shown as purple dots scattered in the middle of the chart, mostly between about 5 and 20 solar masses.</li>
        <li>These masses are noticeably smaller than the LIGO-Virgo black holes above them.</li>
    </ul>
    </li>
    <li>LIGO-Virgo Neutron Stars
    <ul>
        <li>Shown as a small number of orange dots near the bottom of the chart, clustered around 1 to 2 solar masses.</li>
    </ul>
    </li>
    <li>EM Neutron Stars
    <ul>
        <li>Shown as numerous yellow dots along the bottom of the chart, also clustered between about 1 and 2 solar masses.</li>
    </ul>
    </li>
</ul>
<p>Key Trend: Black holes detected through gravitational waves by LIGO-Virgo have consistently higher masses than black holes detected through electromagnetic radiation, including some merged black holes massive enough to challenge existing models, while neutron star masses detected by both methods cluster tightly around 1 to 2 solar masses.</p>
New Figure Caption Text
Gravitational wave mergers detected by LIGO-Virgo have revealed black holes of significantly higher mass than those previously identified through electromagnetic observations, including some so massive that new models may be needed to explain their formation.

New Alt-Text
Chart comparing masses of black holes and neutron stars from LIGO-Virgo and electromagnetic observations.

Original alt-text and caption text (as submitted)
<p>Original alt-text: A graph labeled Masses in the Stellar Graveyard has a vertical y axis showing the solar masses from 1, 2, 5, 10, 20, 40, 80, and 160. The graph shows LIGO-Virgo Neutron Stars, EM Neutron Stars, EM Black Holes, and LIGO-Virgo Black Holes.</p> <p>Original figure caption text: The vertical axis shows the masses for some known black holes and neutron stars. The light blue dots represent black hole mergers seen with LIGO-Virgo, with the individual black holes and the resulting merged black hole connected with an arrow. Note that, as a result of these mergers, we are starting to observe some black holes with masses so large that new ideas may be required to explain them. The purple dots represent black holes seen with electro-magnetic radiation; these have significantly smaller masses. The orange dots represent neutron star mergers seen through the gravitational waves they emit. The yellow dots are neutron stars seen with electro-magnetic radiation.</p>

Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 14.3.2 Gravitational Wave</h2>
<p>Charts of gravitational wave data and an illustration of merging black holes.</p>
<h3>Chart (a)</h3>
<ul>
    <li>Three stacked line charts compare gravitational wave signals measured at two observatories.</li>
    <li>The vertical axis for each chart is labeled Strain (10<sup>-21</sup>), ranging from -1.0 at the bottom to 1.0 at the top, in increments of 0.5.</li>
    <li>The horizontal axis is labeled Time (seconds), ranging from 0.25 on the left to 0.45 on the right, in increments of 0.05.</li>
    <li>Top chart: LIGO Hanford Data, shown in orange, with a thin white Predicted curve overlaid closely matching it.</li>
    <li>Middle chart: LIGO Livingston Data, shown in blue, with a thin white Predicted curve overlaid closely matching it.</li>
    <li>Bottom chart: LIGO Hanford Data, shifted in time, shown in orange, superimposed with LIGO Livingston Data, shown in blue.</li>
    <li>In all three charts, the signal begins as a small, irregular oscillation between about -0.05 and 0.05.</li>
    <li>Around T = 0.35 seconds, the oscillations increase sharply in amplitude and frequency, reaching between -1.0 and 1.0.</li>
    <li>The oscillations reach their fastest, largest swings near T = 0.42 seconds.</li>
    <li>After about T = 0.425 seconds, the oscillations diminish back to their original small amplitude.</li>
    <li>Key Trend: The measured strain at both observatories closely matches the predicted waveform, and the two independent signals align closely with each other once shifted, confirming the detection of a gravitational wave from a black hole merger.</li>
</ul>
<h3>Illustration (b)</h3>
<ul>
    <li>An artist's illustration of two black holes orbiting each other shortly before merging.</li>
    <li>The black holes are shown as two dark circular regions near the center of the image, one larger than the other, close together.</li>
    <li>A faint ring of glowing gas and light surrounds the pair, swept into a swirling, lens-like pattern by their combined gravity.</li>
    <li>The background is filled with a dense field of stars in shades of white, blue, and orange, resembling a star cluster.</li>
    <li>Light from stars behind the black holes is bent into warped, arc-like streaks near the black holes, illustrating gravitational lensing.</li>
</ul>
New Figure Caption Text
The close agreement between the measured strain at Hanford and Livingston and the theoretical prediction in Chart (a) confirmed the detection of gravitational waves, produced by the merging black holes illustrated in (b).

New Alt-Text
Charts of gravitational wave data and an illustration of merging black holes.

Original alt-text and caption text (as submitted)
<p>Original alt-text: Signal Produced by a Gravitational Wave. Panel (a), at top, shows three measurements of a gravitational wave signal. At top is the LIGO Hanford Data, at center is the LIGO Livingston Data and bottom is the combined plot of LIGO Hanford Data (shifted) and LIGO Livingston Data. The vertical axis for each measurement is labeled Strain (10-21), ranging from -1.0 at bottom to 1.0 at top, in increments of 0.5. The horizontal axis is labeled Time (sec), ranging from 0.25 at left to 0.45 at right, in increments of 0.05. Each plot begins at left with a slight oscillation between -0.05 and 0.05 until T = 0.35 sec, when the oscillations increase in amplitude to -1.0 to 1.0. At T = 0.425 sec the oscillations diminish to their original levels. Panel (b), at bottom, shows an artist's impression of two black holes orbiting each other. Of note is the distortion of the light from background stars due to the strong gravitational field of the pair.</p> <p>Original figure caption text: (a) The top panel shows the signal measured at Hanford, Washington; the middle panel shows the signal measured at Livingston, Louisiana. The smoother thin curve in each panel shows the predicted signal, based on the general theory of relativity, produced by the merger of two black holes. The bottom panel shows a superposition of the waves detected at the two LIGO observatories. Note the remarkable agreement of the two independent observations and of the observations with theory. (b) The painting shows an artist's impression of two massive black holes spiraling inward toward an eventual merger.</p>

Here's the complete set for Figure 14.1.1 Classical Collapse.

Original Text (as submitted)
Original alt-text: An astronaut floats above a star as it shrinks.

Original caption text: This illustration depicts the formation of a black hole by classical collapse in three stages. Starting on the left, an astronaut floats near the surface of a massive star-core about to collapse. As the star's mass falls into a smaller sphere in the center picture, the gravity at its surface goes up. In the picture on the right, the mass of the star has collapsed into a sphere so small that the escape velocity exceeds the speed of light. The astronaut is outside of the sphere we call the event horizon and is stretched and squeezed by the strong gravity. The size of the astronaut has been exaggerated compared to the size of the star.

Accessible Description
<h2>Figure 14.1.1 Classical Collapse</h2>
<p>An illustration of a collapsing star forming a black hole.</p>
<h3>Left Panel</h3>
<ul>
    <li>An astronaut floats near the surface of a large, softly glowing blue sphere representing a massive star.</li>
</ul>
<h3>Center Panel</h3>
<ul>
    <li>The star has contracted into a smaller, white sphere.</li>
    <li>The astronaut floats near the surface, appearing about the same size as before.</li>
    <li>Blue arrows point inward toward the sphere from all directions, representing the star's collapse and increasing surface gravity.</li>
</ul>
<h3>Right Panel</h3>
<ul>
    <li>The star has collapsed into a tiny black dot, representing a black hole.</li>
    <li>The astronaut is stretched into a long, thin shape while hovering just above the dot, illustrating the extreme gravitational pull.</li>
    <li>The text No escape appears below the black dot.</li>
</ul>
New Figure Caption Text
As a massive star's core collapses into an ever-smaller volume, its surface gravity increases until the escape velocity exceeds the speed of light, forming the event horizon of a black hole.

New Alt-Text
Astronaut floating above a collapsing star that becomes a black hole.

I've read through the tool set. Before you paste in a figure, a few clarifying questions so I apply the right defaults:

1. Workflow scope — Do you want me to run the full procedure.html pipeline every time (accessible description → caption → alt-text → attribution → figure html), or would you sometimes want just one piece (e.g., only an accessible description)? I'll default to full pipeline unless you say otherwise.

2. Accessible description page link — The figure html needs an acc-desc-link, built from a Workbench-style link you provide (e.g. /Workbench/TextbookTitle/ChapterTitle/SectionNumber:_Accessible_Descriptions). Will you supply this each time, or is there a standing link for the current chapter I should reuse until told otherwise?

3. Default attribution shortcut — For Openstax Astronomy figures, I'll use the default attribution values shown in fig-att/examples.html (License CC BY 4.0, Author Fraknoi et al., Source Openstax Astronomy 2nd ed.) whenever you just say "Openstax" — correct?

4. Missing info — Per procedure.html, if something's missing (figure number, license info, etc.) I should ask you for it rather than guess. I'll do that, but let me know if you'd prefer I flag gaps and proceed with placeholders instead, for speed.

5. Output format — Would you like each figure's output as inline HTML in chat (easy to copy into Libretext), or saved as an .html file per figure? I'll default to inline chat output unless you want files.

Once I have your preferences (or you just want me to use the defaults above), go ahead and paste in the figure, its image/description, and any metadata (license, author, source, figure number/title, etc.), and I'll generate the full set: accessible description, caption text, alt-text, attribution, and final figure HTML.

IDEA Framework Analysis
Using the IDEA Framework (Sept. 2023), the following observations are made:

1. Diverse Authors, Researchers, and Studies (Section 7.4)

Per the IDEA Framework: "Referencing discipline contributors—e.g., researchers, scholars, academics—with backgrounds like those of students both validates and affirms the students as student-scholars and invites them into the academic conversation."

The text references the following scientists by name:

Joseph Taylor and Russell Hulse (1974 pulsar discovery, 1993 Nobel Prize)
Rainer Weiss (MIT), Kip Thorne and Barry Barish (Caltech) (2017 Nobel Prize)
Einstein (general theory of relativity)
All named scientists are presented as Western, and the text does not acknowledge the contributions of scientists from diverse backgrounds to gravitational wave research. The LIGO-Virgo collaboration is a large international effort involving scientists from many countries and backgrounds. Consider adding brief acknowledgment of the global and diverse nature of this collaboration. For example:

The LIGO Scientific Collaboration includes over 1,000 scientists from more than 20 countries.
Nergis Mavalvala, a Pakistani-American physicist at MIT, made significant contributions to LIGO's development and has spoken publicly about her identity as a queer woman of color in physics.
2. Concept Naming (Section 7.4, STEM guidance)

Per the IDEA Framework: "Referencing theory and concepts by the scientist's or theorist's name elevates the person over the concept. When appropriate, shift attention to the concept by using descriptions that signify the reason the concept is important."

The text references "Einstein's general theory of relativity" and "Kepler's third law" by name multiple times. Consider whether some of these references could be rephrased to focus on the concept (e.g., "the law of orbital periods" instead of "Kepler's third law") where appropriate.

3. Prior Knowledge Assumptions (Section 7.4, IDEA Framework)

Per the IDEA Framework: "Be mindful when creating exercises that require specific knowledge, context, or frame of reference."

The analogy comparing gravitational wave astronomy to "the era when silent films were replaced by movies with sound" assumes familiarity with film history. While this is a widely known cultural reference, it may not be universally accessible to all students. Consider providing a brief explanation or an alternative analogy.

No paragraph text was highlighted in red, as no content was found to rise to the level of requiring a red highlight per the IDEA Framework guidelines. The orange highlight for the spelling error is noted above.

Recommended Changes (Non-Text / IDEA Framework)
Figure XXX (LIGO Aerial Photograph): The credit reads "Caltech/MIT/LIGO Laboratory." Consider adding a note about the international and diverse nature of the LIGO collaboration in the caption or surrounding text, per IDEA Framework Section 7.4.
Figure XXX (Signal Produced by a Gravitational Wave): The credit reads "SXS" (Simulating eXtreme Spacetimes). Consider adding a brief explanation of what SXS is, to make the credit more accessible to students.
Figure XXX (Masses in the Stellar Graveyard): The credit includes "Frank Elavsky, Aaron Geller/Northwestern." This is a good example of crediting individual contributors. No changes recommended.
"Alternative description of Figure XXX" in all three figcaptions is placeholder text and should be replaced with actual alternative descriptions for accessibility.
Figure XXX (Masses in the Stellar Graveyard): The alt text for this figure is minimal. Consider expanding it to describe the data shown in the graph more fully, to support students with visual impairments.
LISA launch date: The text states LISA is planned for launch in 2034. This information should be verified for currency, as launch schedules may have changed.
LIGO India and KAGRA observing run: The text states the first four-detector observing run "is scheduled to begin in the summer of 2022." This is now in the past (relative to the current date of August 8, 2026) and should be updated to reflect current status.
Recommended Section Headers
H2: Gravitational Waves

Introductory paragraph: This section introduces gravitational waves—ripples in spacetime predicted by Einstein's general theory of relativity—and describes how scientists have worked to detect and study them, opening a new era of multi-messenger astronomy.

H3: Indirect Evidence: Proof from a Pulsar
The Hulse-Taylor binary pulsar
Orbital decay and the 1993 Nobel Prize
H3: Building a Gravitational Wave Observatory
The LIGO experiment and its design
Technological challenges and development
H3: First Detections: Black Hole Mergers
The first gravitational wave detection in 2015
The LIGO-Virgo catalog of merger events
Unexpected black hole mass ranges
H3: Neutron Star Mergers and Multi-Messenger Astronomy
The 2017 neutron star merger event
Gamma-ray bursts and heavy element production
The importance of multi-messenger observations
H3: The Future of Gravitational Wave Astronomy
Expanding the global detector network
LISA and space-based detection of supermassive black hole mergers

IDEA Framework Flags
Paragraph text highlighted for IDEA review: No paragraph text was highlighted in red in the processed HTML above. However, the following concerns are noted based on the IDEA Framework:

Diverse Authors, Researchers, and Studies (Section 7.4 of IDEA Framework): The text references "Einstein's general theory of relativity" and "Kepler's law" without acknowledging the contributions of diverse scientists to these fields or to black hole research. Per the IDEA Framework, "referencing discipline contributors with backgrounds like those of students both validates and affirms the students as student-scholars." Consider adding brief references to the contributions of scientists from diverse backgrounds, such as:

Subrahmanyan Chandrasekhar (Indian-American astrophysicist, Nobel Prize winner), whose work on stellar evolution laid groundwork for black hole theory.
Andrea Ghez (American astrophysicist), who co-discovered the supermassive black hole at the center of the Milky Way and received the Nobel Prize in Physics in 2020.
The Event Horizon Telescope collaboration, which included scientists from many countries and backgrounds.
Applications, Examples, and Problem Scenarios (IDEA Framework): The phrase "having some of their stellar companions for lunch" uses a culturally specific food metaphor. While this is a common informal expression in astronomy writing, per the IDEA Framework, it is worth considering whether such colloquialisms are universally accessible to all students.

Naming conventions: The text notes that "there is no standard way of naming these candidates" and explains the naming conventions used. This is helpful context for students who may not be familiar with astronomical catalog systems.

Recommended Changes (Non-Text / IDEA Framework)
Figure XXX (Binary Black Hole illustration): The figure caption references "ESO/L. Calçada" as the credit. Consider adding a brief note about the European Southern Observatory (ESO) and its international collaborative nature, to highlight the global and diverse community of scientists involved in astronomical research.
Table XXX (Black Hole Candidates): The table lists only catalog designations and physical properties. Consider adding a column or footnote noting the discovery missions or teams, which could highlight the international and collaborative nature of black hole research.
Figure descriptions: Ensure the existing alt text for the Binary Black Hole figure is sufficient for screen reader accessibility. The current alt text is descriptive and appears adequate, but consider having it reviewed by an accessibility specialist.
"Alternative description of Figure XXX" in the figcaption appears to be placeholder text. This should be replaced with an actual alternative description for accessibility purposes.

Per the IDEA Framework (Section 7.4), consider adding references to contributions from scientists of diverse backgrounds. For example, Indian astrophysicist Subrahmanyan Chandrasekhar made foundational contributions to the theory of stellar collapse and black holes.

Paragraph Text — Highlighted in Red
No paragraph text was flagged in red for this pass. However, see the recommended changes below regarding IDEA concerns.

Recommended Changes for IDEA Compliance
Illustrations and Photos: The image of Edwin Hubble (figure-223) shows only one person. Consider adding or replacing images with more diverse representations of astronomers, including women and people of color who contributed to the field (e.g., Henrietta Leavitt, Cecilia Payne-Gaposchkin, Vera Rubin).
Diverse Authors and Researchers: The text does mention Abd al-Rahman al-Sufi, Georges Lemaître, and Alexander Friedmann, which is a positive step toward representing diverse contributors. However, the contributions of women astronomers — particularly Henrietta Leavitt, whose work on Cepheid variables was foundational to Hubble's distance measurements — are mentioned only briefly. Consider expanding on Leavitt's contributions.
Gender-Inclusive Language: The text uses "his" and male pronouns exclusively when referring to astronomers. Where the gender of a historical figure is known, this is acceptable, but consider adding a note acknowledging that many contributors to astronomy have been women and people from diverse backgrounds.
Applications and Examples: The example using "65 miles/hour" as a velocity reference assumes familiarity with the imperial system of measurement. Consider adding or substituting a metric equivalent (e.g., 100 km/h) to be more inclusive of international students.
"Growing up" assumption: The opening sentence of one paragraph — "Growing up at a time when the Hubble Space Telescope orbits above our heads..." — assumes a shared generational experience. Consider revising to be more inclusive of students of all ages and backgrounds.
Figure for Lemaître: The image of Lemaître writing on a chalkboard is a good inclusion of a non-American scientist. Consider adding similar images for other contributors mentioned in the text.

6. Recommended Changes (Including Non-Text Material)
Abd al-Rahman al-Sufi (IDEA – Diverse Authors & Researchers, Section 7.4): The sentence introduces al-Sufi in a way that leads with his religious and ethnic identity ("a Muslim astronomer from Persia") before his scholarly contributions. Consider reframing to lead with his scholarly role, e.g., "Abd al-Rahman al-Sufi, a tenth-century Persian astronomer and scholar, was the first to recognize..." This better reflects the IDEA Framework's guidance on centering contributions rather than identity markers.
Images of people (IDEA – Illustrations and Photos, Section 7.1): The only images of a person in this section are of Edwin Hubble and Georges Lemaître, both of whom are white men. Consider adding images of other contributors to cosmology from underrepresented groups, such as Henrietta Leavitt, Cecilia Payne-Gaposchkin, or Vera Rubin, to better reflect the diversity of contributors to the field.
Galaxy images (IDEA – Illustrations and Photos, Section 7.1): The Whirlpool Galaxy figures lack consistent, detailed captions identifying the telescope or instrument used for the second image. Consider adding this information for completeness and accuracy.
Duplicate "Hubble's Law" H2 headers: There are two separate <h2> sections both titled "Hubble's Law" (one with class lt-phys-64239 and one without). These should be merged or differentiated with distinct titles to avoid confusion.
Table data error: The second row of the Hubble parameter comparison table shows "500 km/s/Mpc" in the Age of the Universe column. This should read "1.92 billion years." This has been highlighted in orange but should be corrected by the editor.
Henrietta Leavitt (IDEA – Diverse Authors & Researchers, Section 7.4): Henrietta Leavitt is mentioned only briefly as the pioneer of the Cepheid distance technique. Consider expanding her contribution with a sentence or two, as her work was foundational and she is a notable woman scientist whose contributions are often underrepresented.
Father Georges Lemaître description: The text refers to Lemaître as "Father Georges Lemaître, a Catholic Belgian Priest" in one section and "a Belgian Catholic priest" in another. Consider standardizing the description and leading with his scientific credentials (astronomer, physicist) before his religious role, for consistency with IDEA principles.
"Growing up" framing (lt-phys-64824 paragraph): The opening phrase "Growing up at a time when the Hubble Space Telescope orbits above our heads..." assumes a shared generational experience that may not apply to all students. Consider revising to be more inclusive of students of different ages and backgrounds.
"Most of us are used to speeds in miles per hour" (Hubble's Law section): This assumes a US-centric experience. Most of the world uses kilometers per hour. Consider revising to acknowledge both systems.

6. Recommended Changes (Non-text and IDEA Framework)
Spelling highlight needed: "mid-1990's" should be "mid-1990s" — please highlight in orange and review.
Grammar highlight needed: "more-in agreement" in the Hubble Space Telescope figure caption should be "more in agreement" — please highlight in orange and review.
Illustrations and Photos (IDEA 7.1): The images currently included show Lord Rosse's sketch of the Whirlpool Galaxy, the Whirlpool Galaxy photograph, the Andromeda Galaxy, Georges Lemaître at a chalkboard, Edwin Hubble at a telescope, and two data graphs. The human images (Lemaître and Hubble) depict only white men. Consider adding images of diverse astronomers who contributed to this field, such as Abd al-Rahman al-Sufi (mentioned in the text), Henrietta Leavitt, or other astronomers from underrepresented groups, to better reflect the diversity of contributors to astronomy.
Diverse Authors and Researchers (IDEA 7.8): The text does mention Abd al-Rahman al-Sufi and Georges Lemaître, which is positive. However, the contributions of Henrietta Leavitt — whose Cepheid variable work is cited as foundational to Hubble's distance measurements — are mentioned only briefly and without context about her identity or the barriers she faced. Consider expanding on her contribution and background.
Duplicate H2 header "Hubble's Law": There are two H2 headers both titled "Hubble's Law" — one with class lt-phys-64239 and one without a class. This may confuse readers and should be differentiated or consolidated.
Duplicate content: The Hubble's Law equation v=Hodv=Ho​d and its rearrangement appear multiple times across sections. Consider consolidating to avoid repetition.
Figure captions for Whirlpool Galaxy figures: The second figure caption reads only "A photograph taken by the Hubble Space Telescope." This lacks sufficient descriptive context. Consider expanding to include the galaxy's name and catalog number for accessibility and educational value.
Alt text review: The alt text for the figures appears adequate, but the Lemaître chalkboard image alt text ("Georges Lemaître is shown writing on a chalkboard") could be more descriptive about the context.
Table error: The second row of the Age of the Universe table shows "500 km/s/Mpc" in the Age column instead of the calculated age value of 1.92 billion years. This appears to be a content error and should be corrected.
Gender-inclusive language (IDEA 7.2): The text uses "he" pronouns throughout when referring to Hubble, Lemaître, and others. Since these are historical figures, this is appropriate, but consider adding a brief note acknowledging the broader, more diverse community of scientists involved in these discoveries.
